# TO THE READER
## GREETINGS TO THE WELL-WISHING READER <note>original: "BENEVOLO S.," an abbreviation for *Benevolo Salutem*, a standard Latin greeting wishing the reader health and prosperity.</note>

Here for you is the *History of the Templars* <note>original: "Historiam Templariorum"</note>, perhaps recalled to memory not without purpose. It is composed more often from the words of others than my own, a method chosen neither without precedent nor without reason. For you may see the thoughts, spirit, faith, rituals, and customs of the <term>Ancients<note>In this context, the author refers to the original medieval chroniclers and witnesses of the Crusades.</note></term> more reliably in their own words than you might learn from later interpreters. I have expanded the work with ecclesiastical observations concerning the *vows of monks, the dignity of <term>patriarchs<note>High-ranking bishops in the early and Eastern church; the Patriarch of Jerusalem was a key figure in the Templars' history.</note></term>, the clothing of religious persons, the generosity shown toward <term>holy orders<note>Groups such as the Templars or Hospitallers who took religious vows while performing specific duties, like military service.</note></term>, and so forth, so that...*

<summary>In this preface, the author introduces his history of the Knights Templar, explaining that he prefers quoting original medieval sources directly to preserve their authentic spirit. He also highlights his additional research into monastic life, church hierarchy, and the charitable giving that supported religious orders.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, monasticism, primary sources, patriarchs, religious vows, church history, historiography</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface. The catchword "SUM-" from the previous page matches "summa" in the text below, though the transcription begins mid-sentence, continuing the thought that the variety of topics is intended for the reader's enjoyment.</meta>

...perhaps your mind may be delighted by this useful variety. If, however, you wish to read the fates of our soldiers <note>original: "fata nostrorum militum"; the author refers to the Knights Templar as "our" soldiers in a historical or religious sense.</note> in an uninterrupted sequence, the subjoined summary of sections <term>summary of sections<note>A table of contents or index provided to help the reader follow the main historical narrative without the author's topical interruptions.</note></term> will lead you to that goal. I only ask that, while reading of the superstitions of the <term>Ancients<note>The author is referring to the medieval people of the Crusading era.</note></term>, their false opinions, rash vows, and foolish labors, you might at the same time reflect with thanksgiving on the happiness of these later times, which has arisen from the Light of the Gospel <note>original: "Luce Evangelii"; likely a reference to the Protestant Reformation's emphasis on returning to biblical texts.</note> restored to Christians. In this way, you will not regret having read this little book, nor will I regret having written it.

->Farewell.<-

<summary>The author concludes his preface by explaining how to navigate the book's narrative and encourages the reader to compare the "superstitions" of the medieval Templars with the enlightened Christian era of the author's own time.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, historiography, Light of the Gospel, medieval superstitions, table of contents, religious history</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the formal narrative history of the Templars. While the previous page concluded a detailed table of contents and summary of sections, this page introduces the author's primary argument: that religious and military orders were created to bolster the power of the Pope.</meta>

# HISTORY
## OF THE TEMPLARS.

<margin>
§. 1.
*Religious Orders.*
</margin>
Those who possess even a moderate knowledge of ecclesiastical affairs <note>The author uses the term "ecclesiastical affairs" to refer to the history and governance of the Christian Church.</note> cannot be ignorant of the fact that the power of the Bishop of Rome <note>original: "Romani Epifcopi," referring to the Pope.</note>—by which he has for so long commanded the faith of the Christian people toward many grave errors and a superstitious and false form of worship—has been both strengthened and expanded by the various *Orders* of Monks and Knights. Just as the Church of Jesus Christ hopes, with good reason, that that seat of iniquity <note>This "seat of iniquity" refers to the Papacy; the author writes from a strongly Protestant perspective, viewing the Roman Catholic Church as an opponent to the "throne of God."</note>, which is opposed to the throne of God and our King, will one day not only be shaken but entirely overturned: so also we have no doubt that these irreligious Orders, the pillars of the Roman Chair <note>original: "Romanæ Cathedræ," also known as the Holy See or the office of the Pope.</note>, partly [by their] origin—

<meta>catchword: men-</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the history of the Templars by framing religious and military orders as strategic tools used to expand the power and "superstitions" of the Papacy. The author, writing from a Protestant viewpoint, expresses a desire for the eventual downfall of these institutions.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, Papacy, Bishop of Rome, religious orders, ecclesiastical history, Protestant Reformation perspective</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction from page 1, completing the sentence regarding the fate of religious and military orders. It transitions from a general critique of monasticism to the specific historical example of the Knights Templar.</meta>

-># HISTORY<-

[e]mending, they are to be withdrawn; others are to be fundamentally destroyed and abolished.

<margin>
§. 2.
Once to be reformed.
</margin>
Indeed, hope for the reform of monastic life <note>original: "vitæ Monasticæ"</note> in former times was suggested not only by its early origin—which was innocent and suited both to the piety of individuals and the common benefit of the Church—but also by the history of the past century. From this history, it is clear that religious and wise men, when the Gospel was brought back from darkness into light <note>This phrase "Euangelium è tenebris in lucem" is a classic Reformation-era slogan (Post Tenebras Lux) referring to the Protestant movement's belief that they were restoring the true teachings of the Bible.</note>, deliberated on transforming monasteries into colleges of wisdom and seminaries for the Church <note>The author refers to the practice of many Protestant reformers who converted former Catholic monasteries into schools or universities.</note>.

<margin>
§. 3.
Or to be abolished, like the Templars.
</margin>
However, a great example of an entire Order being abolished by the Pope himself and by Catholic Kings—despite being formidable in both its numbers and its wealth—is provided by the *Templar* knights <note>original: "Templarii milites"</note>. As we are about to encompass their highly memorable history in this scholarly dissertation, we shall briefly relate their origin, progress, and end, based on faithful historical records <note>original: "fidelibus rerum monumentis"</note> and interspersed throughout with ecclesiastical observations.

<note>Vocabulary used in this section: Monastic (Monastica), Church (Ecclesia), Gospel (Euangelium), Templars (Templarii), Pope (Papa), Catholic Kings (Reges Catholici).</note>

<meta>catchword: Ori-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while some monastic institutions were originally intended for reform into educational seminaries, the Knights Templar serve as a primary historical example of an order that was completely abolished by the authorities. This page sets the stage for a detailed scholarly account of the Templars' rise and fall.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, monastic reform, Protestant Reformation, ecclesiastical history, abolition of orders, Catholic Kings</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general introduction of the previous page into the specific history of the Knights Templar, focusing on the religious practice of pilgrimage as the Order's root cause.</meta>

->### OF THE TEMPLARS.<-

<margin>
§. 4. Origin of the Templars and religious pilgrimages.
</margin>
Therefore, religious pilgrimages—most frequently undertaken to Palestine during that time by superstitious men <note>The author likely uses "superstitious" from a post-Reformation perspective, viewing the medieval obsession with holy relics and physical sites with skepticism.</note>—gave rise to this military and sacred Order. The first founders of the Order took it upon themselves to defend these travelers with weapons against ambushes and robberies.

<margin>
§. 5. Begun in the 4th Century.
</margin>
Indeed, the custom of visiting places regarded as holy took hold in the Church quite early. Concerning Cletus, a Bishop of Rome <note>Saint Cletus, also known as Anacletus, was traditionally the third Pope, serving in the late 1st century AD.</note>, Rolewinck <note>Werner Rolewinck (1425–1502) was a German Carthusian monk and an influential historian; his work was one of the most popular history books of the late Middle Ages.</note> writes under the year 84 AD: *He approved of pilgrimages to the saints, saying: that a visit to Saint Peter is more effective for salvation than a two-year fast. Furthermore, he excommunicated those who hinder or discourage such visits.* <note>original: "Peregrinationes ad sanctos approbavit dicens: quod validior est ad salutem sancti Petri visitatio, quam duorum annorum jejunium. Excommunicavit insuper, qui hujusmodi visitationes impediunt, aut dissuadent."</note> However, although this testimony from a more recent writer, which is not supported by any authority from the ancients, does not deserve our trust; nevertheless, it cannot be denied that once Helena, the mother of Constantine the Great, visited Jerusalem—which she did in the year 326 AD—her footsteps 

<summary>This page identifies the origins of the Knights Templar in the protection of religious pilgrims traveling to the Holy Land. It also traces the historical roots of Christian pilgrimage back to the 4th-century journey of Empress Helena, while questioning earlier claims of the practice.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, pilgrimage, Palestine, Pope Cletus, Werner Rolewinck, Empress Helena, Jerusalem, 4th century</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the history of pilgrimages to the Holy Land. The previous page ended with a mention of Empress Helena visiting Jerusalem, and this page picks up with the "traces" she left for others to follow.</meta>

->## HISTORY<-

...traces that many Christians, not only men but also women, followed; as examples frequently appear in the works of Sulpicius Severus <note>A Christian writer of the 4th century, best known for his biography of Saint Martin of Tours.</note>, Jerome <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the priest and scholar who translated the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note>, Gregory of Nyssa <note>A 4th-century theologian and one of the Cappadocian Fathers.</note>, Paul the Deacon <note>An 8th-century Benedictine monk and historian of the Lombards.</note>, Landulfus Sagax <note>An 11th-century historian who continued the works of Paul the Deacon.</note>, and other authors of those times.

<margin>
§. 6. Most frequent in the 11th century.
</margin>
But this "traveling religion" <note>original: "ambulatoria religio" — a descriptive term for the practice of pilgrimage as a way of life.</note> was taken up by the common people especially in the 11th century; concerning this, I think it worthwhile to quote the words of *Rodulfus Glaber* <note>Rodulfus Glaber (c. 985–1047) was a monk and chronicler of the Middle Ages, famous for his accounts of the turn of the first millennium.</note> from *Ussher’s On the State and Succession of the Church, chapter 4, section 11.* <note>James Ussher (1581–1656) was the Archbishop of Armagh, famous for his chronological history of the world.</note> He says: *When certain of the prognosticators <note>original: "solicitatoribus" — likely referring to religious counselors or those who interpreted the "signs of the times."</note> who lived at that time were asked by many what such a great gathering of people to Jerusalem signified—something unheard of in the previous century—some answered cautiously enough: that it portended nothing else than the arrival of that most ruinous Antichrist, who, according to divine authority, is expected to appear near the end of this century.*

From its very first origin, the Christian Church was divided into the congregation and those who presided over the congregation. <meta>This page continues the historical overview of church hierarchy, specifically how the early, fluid roles of leadership solidified into the distinct offices of Bishop, Presbyter, and Priest, separating the "Clergy" from the "Laity."</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>OF THE TEMPLARS.</header>

...new distinctions among the members of the Church, as well as specific titles, began to emerge. For the **Bishop** <note>original Latin: *Episcopus*, from the Greek for "overseer."</note> came to be distinguished from the title of **presbyter** or elder, and was held in higher honor; and generally, the ministers of the Church, whether they were Bishops or presbyters, were called **priests** <note>original: *sacerdotes*</note>. Soon, they were further distinguished by the title of **Clergy** <note>original: *Clerici*</note> from the general assembly of the people, which—though Peter called them the *lot* <note>original Greek: κλῆρος (*klēros*). In the New Testament, this referred to the whole people as God’s "inheritance," but the author notes that the term eventually became restricted to the ordained ministry.</note>—came to be known by the name of **Laity** <note>original: *Laïci*, from the Greek *laos*, meaning "the people" as distinguished from their leaders.</note>. 

**Ignatius, *Epistle to the Smyrnaeans*:** *I greet the bishop, worthy of God, and the most God-befitting presbytery, and my fellow-servants the deacons.* <note>original Greek: "Ἀσπάζομαι τὸν ἀξιόθεον, ἐπίσκοπον καὶ θεοπρεπέστατον πρεσβυτέριον, τοὺς συνδούλους μου διακόνους." Ignatius of Antioch (c. 35–107 AD) was an early Christian writer whose letters are among the first to emphasize a clear three-tier hierarchy of bishops, priests, and deacons.</note> 

**Tertullian, *On the Exhortation to Chastity*:** *We shall be foolish if we think that what is not permitted to priests is permitted to the laity.* <note>original Latin: "Vani erimus, si putaverimus, quod sacerdotibus non liceat, laïcis licere."</note> 

**Cyprian, *Epistle 52*:** *Moreover, Cornelius was made Bishop by the judgment of God and His Christ, by the testimony of almost all the clergy, by the vote of the people who were then present, and by the assembly of ancient priests and good men.* <note>original Latin: "Factus est autem Cornelius Episcopus de Dei & Christi ejus judicio, de Clericorum pene omnium testimonio, de plebis, quæ tum affuit, suffragio, & de sacerdotum antiquorum & bonorum virorum collegio." This refers to the election of Pope Cornelius in 251 AD.</note> 

**Jerome, *Book 2, Epistle 14*:** *If "cleros" in Greek is called "sors" [lot] in Latin, they are for that reason called [clerics...]* <note>original Latin: "Si cleros græce sors latine appellatur, propterea vocantur..." Jerome is explaining the etymology of the word "Clergy," suggesting they are those whose "lot" or portion is the service of God.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 13 regarding the evolution of the Church hierarchy, moving from the general distinction between clergy and laity to the specific development of monastic orders and "Canons" (clergy living under a communal rule).</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>HISTORY</header>

**Clerics** <note>original Latin: *Clerici*</note> are so called either because they are of the "lot" or portion of the Lord, or because the Lord Himself is their lot—that is, the portion belonging to the Clerics. <note>This refers to the biblical idea that the clergy, like the ancient Levites, find their inheritance in God rather than in worldly property.</note> Then, in the age of Paul of Thebes, Anthony, Basil, and Hilarion, there were born in the Church the **Monks**, <note>These figures (3rd–4th centuries AD) are the traditional founders of Christian monasticism. Paul and Anthony are associated with the Egyptian desert, Basil with communal living in the East, and Hilarion with the Palestinian tradition.</note> a class intermediate between the Clergy and the Laity. These individuals lived first in solitudes and soon after in cities, segregated from human society for the sake of a stricter life. 

Furthermore, in the times that followed, those among the Clerics—who were either destined for the ministry of the Church or already established in it—began to share a common table and dormitory. They lived near the temple, either in the Bishop’s residence <note>original: *Episcopio*</note> or in buildings adjacent to it. Bound by certain rules that were somewhat more relaxed than the Monastic life, they devoted themselves to communal sacred studies under the discipline of the Bishop or a provost. These men obtained the name of **Canons**. <note>original: *Canonici*. The term refers to clergy who live according to a "canon" or rule, but who serve a specific cathedral or church rather than living in a secluded monastery.</note>

**Charlemagne, *Capitularies*, Book 1, Chapter 73:**
*Likewise, those who enter the Clericate—which we call the Canonical life—it is our will that they live canonically according to their rule in every way...* <note>original Latin: "Similiter, qui ad Clericatum accedunt, quod nos nominamus Canonicam vitam, volumus ut illi canonice secundum suam regulam omnimodis vi[vant]..." The text ends mid-word, following the catchword.</note>

<meta>catchword: live,</meta>

<summary>This page traces the historical emergence of Monks and Canons as distinct groups within the Church structure, highlighting their communal living arrangements and the influence of Charlemagne's laws on "Canonical" life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 14 regarding the origins of the "Canons," specifically addressing the etymology of the name and the eventual split between Regular and Secular Canons.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>OF THE TEMPLARS</header> <note>The header refers to the "Templars," but the content of this page focuses on the history of Canons. It is likely part of a broader study of religious and military orders.</note>

...they observe, and their Bishop governs their life, *just as an Abbot governs his monks.* From this we may gather that they were so named from the "Canons" or rules they were meant to observe, although Charles du Fresne <note>Charles du Fresne, Sieur du Cange (1610–1688), was a renowned French philologist and historian of the Middle Ages, famous for his "Glossary of Medieval Latin."</note> suggests in his *Glossary* that they were *named from the "grain-canon" <note>original: *canone frumentario*</note> or from the ecclesiastical "baskets" <note>original: *sportulis*—this refers to distributions of food or money.</note> and the annual or monthly stipends which they received from Church revenues.* The same author more correctly observes that the name of "Canons" was already known in the age of Gregory of Tours <note>A 6th-century bishop and historian whose "History of the Franks" is a primary source for the Merovingian period.</note>, since he writes in his *History* (Book 10, Chapter 31) that Baudin, Bishop of the Church of Tours, established a "table for the Canons" <note>original: *mensam Canonicorum*. This refers to a communal fund or endowment specifically set aside to provide food for the clergy.</note>.

<margin>
§. 16.
Who are the Regular and Secular Canons?
</margin>

However, as the corruption of the age increased, and as luxury and license began to dominate the Church, most Canons continued to neglect the rules prescribed for them, being brought back to the canonical life neither by the laws of Kings nor by the decrees of Synods. Finally, thought was given to restoring the ancient institution, and the name of ***Regular Canons*** <note>original: *Canonicorum Regularium*. From the Latin *regula*, meaning "rule." These were clergy who lived a communal, monastic-style life.</note> was given to those who [followed] the canons of their order more diligently...

<summary>This page discusses the historical origins of the term "Canon," debating whether it derives from the rules (canons) they followed or the grain rations (canons) they received. It also introduces the decline of clerical discipline that led to the creation of the "Regular Canons" as a reform movement.</summary>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>HISTORY</header>

Clergy <note>original: *Clerici*</note> [are so named] either because they belong to the Lord's "lot," or because the Lord himself is the "lot"—that is, the portion—of the Clergy. <note>The term "Clergy" derives from the Greek *kleros*, meaning "lot" or "inheritance," referring to the tribe of Levi in the Old Testament who had no land but had the Lord as their inheritance.</note> Next, in the era of Paul of Thebes, Anthony, Basil, and Hilarion <note>These figures are the "Desert Fathers," the 3rd and 4th-century pioneers of Christian monasticism.</note>, *Monks* were born into the Church—a class intermediate between the Clergy and the Laity, who lived separated from human society for the sake of a stricter life, first in the wilderness and soon after in the cities as well.

Furthermore, in the times that followed, those among the Clergy who were either destined for the ministry of the Church or already established in it, made use of a common table and dormitory because of their service to the temple—living either in the Bishop's residence <note>original: *Episcopio*</note> itself or in buildings adjacent to it. Bound by certain rules, yet living a life slightly freer than the Monastic one, they engaged in communal sacred studies under the discipline of the Bishop or a provost; these obtained the name of *Canons*. 

Charlemagne <note>Charles the Great (742–814), King of the Franks and Holy Roman Emperor, who standardized church discipline.</note>, *Capitularies*, Book 1, Chapter 73: 

*Likewise, those who enter the Clergy, which we call the "Canonical life," we desire that they live canonically according to their rule in every way.*

<vocab>Clergy, Monks, Canons, Charlemagne, Bishop's residence, Canonical life</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the origins of the Clergy, Monks, and Canons, explaining how "Canons" emerged as a specific group of clergy living under a shared rule and communal roof, distinct from both the laity and the stricter monastic orders.</summary>

...[they] observe, and their Bishop shall govern their life, *just as an Abbot governs the monks*. <note>This continues a quote from Charlemagne's laws regarding the behavior of the clergy.</note> From this it can be inferred that they were so named from the "Canons" <term>canon: a rule or law of the Church</term> or rules to be observed, even though Charles du Fresne <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange (1610–1688), was a giant of historical linguistics whose "Glossary" of Medieval Latin is still used today.</note> in his *Glossary* thinks they were named from the *grain ration* <note>original: *canone frumentario*</note> or from *ecclesiastical baskets* <note>original: *sportulis* - referring to small gifts or fees given to officials</note> and annual or monthly stipends which they received from church revenues. <margin>§. 16. Who are the Regular and Secular Canons?</margin>

However, when the corruption of the age increased, and as luxury and license began to dominate the Church, many Canons could not be drawn back to the canonical life—neither by the laws of Kings nor the decrees of Synods—and they continued to neglect the rules prescribed for them. Finally, thought was given to restoring the old institution, and the name of "Regular Canons" was given to those who more diligently [followed] the canons... <note>original: *canones* - The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page.</note>

### ->HISTORY<-

...that same rule <note>original: "eâdem regula"</note> (du Fresne understands this to mean the *Rule of Saint Augustine*) they served the Lord together from their first institution in humility and poverty, devoting themselves to the divine offices, to readings, and to prayers. Serving as soldiers in the camps of the Lord, they took sober food together in the refectory <note>The communal dining hall of a monastery or religious house.</note>, and rested at night in the same dormitory in chaste beds.

And in chapter 21: Those, however, who are called Regular Canons <note>Clergy who live in a community under a specific religious rule (*regula*), as opposed to "secular" clergy who live out in the world.</note>, holding the rule of the **Blessed Augustine** as the foundation of their religious life, walk a middle and royal path. According to the original observances of the regular institutions, they use <term>shirts and breeches</term> <note>original: "camisiis & femoralibus." These were the standard undergarments of the time; "camisia" refers to a linen shirt or tunic.</note>, <term>surplices and furs</term> <note>original: "superpellitiis & pellibus." A surplice is a white liturgical vestment; the furs were often worn underneath for warmth in cold churches.</note>, and mattresses and linens. They do not take off their shirts and breeches at night; returning to their chambers after **Matins** <note>The first prayer service of the day, traditionally held in the very early hours of the morning or late at night.</note>, they sleep for the sake of rest. They do not exceed the number of nine lessons in the night office; they eat meat three days a week, and on other days they eat fish, eggs, and cheese in the refectory.

<page-num>19</page-num>

### ->THE TEMPLARS<-

B 2

It was pleasing to cite these matters more extensively <note>Referring to the lengthy quotations from the historian James of Vitry on the previous pages.</note> so that it might be clear exactly what sort of institution of the **Regular Canons** <note>Priests living in a community under a religious rule, distinguishing them from "secular" priests who lived independently in the world.</note> was followed by the Templars.

<margin>§. 18. The fable regarding Augustine’s rule.</margin>
As for the *Rule of Augustine*, it is an old fable invented by monks for the glory of their order, suggesting that this pious **Doctor of the Church of Hippo** <note>Saint Augustine (354–430 AD), one of the most influential theologians in Western Christianity.</note> prescribed a specific way of living for his brothers; however, **Erasmus** <note>Desiderius Erasmus (1466–1536), a famous humanist scholar who was often skeptical of medieval traditions.</note> mocks this idea, and **Polydore Vergil** <note>An Italian historian (c. 1470–1555) who wrote about the origins of religious customs.</note> could not bring himself to believe it. Anyone who wishes to do so may read the chapters of that rule in **Hospinian's** <note>Rodolph Hospinian (1547–1626), a Swiss Protestant author who wrote a critical history of monastic orders.</note> *History of the Origin of Monks*, book 6, chapter 2. It is true that Augustine lived with students of theology who, once they had grown in their knowledge of sacred things, were assigned as ministers to the Church of Hippo or other churches at the request of the Bishops; but as learned men have observed, he never called himself a monk, and **Possidius** <note>A close friend and the original biographer of Saint Augustine.</note> writes that he lived according to no rule other than that of the **Apostles** <note>Referring to the communal lifestyle of the first Christians described in the New Testament Book of Acts.</note>.

<margin>§. 19. The three vows of the Templars. Triple poverty.</margin>
Furthermore, the *vows* by which they bound themselves in the manner of the Regular Canons were those of poverty, celibacy, and obe-

...of obedience. "In the manner of the **Regular Canons** <note>Priests who lived in a community according to a religious rule (regula), distinguishing them from 'secular' priests who lived in the world.</note> in obedience and chastity, and without personal property, they were to fight for the Highest King," as written by **Jacques de Vitry** <note>original: "Jac. de Vitr. hist. Hier. c. 65." Jacques de Vitry (c. 1160–1240) was a Bishop and historian who provided eyewitness accounts of the Crusades.</note> in his *History of Jerusalem*, chapter 65.

And **Bernard** <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), the influential Cistercian abbot who wrote the "Exhortation to the Knights of the Temple," which helped define the Templars' spiritual mission.</note> in his *Exhortation to the Knights*, chapter 5, states: "They honor the temple of God with eager and sincere service, offering continuous devotion within it—not indeed, after the rite of the ancients, the flesh of livestock, but truly peaceful sacrifices: brotherly love, devoted submission, and voluntary poverty."

Now, there is a threefold poverty observed by clergy and monks:

The **Highest** <note>original: "Altissima"</note>, when we possess nothing either personally or in common—neither fields, nor a house, nor revenues, nor livestock, nor clothes, nor books, nor any piece of household furniture.

The **Middle** <note>original: "Media"</note>, in which we possess nothing personally, yet we hold certain things in common, such as books, clothes, and daily food.

And finally the **Lowest** <note>original: "Infima"</note>, in which some things are held both personally and in common, but only those things which natural necessity requires for food and clothing—

life requires, it is permitted to have such things as clothing, books, stipends, estates and some fields, and other things which pertain to the maintenance of life. And from the beginning, the **Templars** professed a **middle degree of poverty** <note>original: "mediam... paupertatem"; referring to the second degree defined on the previous page where nothing is owned personally, but resources are held in common.</note>, for since they possessed nothing of their own, they first sustained themselves on collected alms; soon, however—as we shall hear below—they acquired immense riches, as superstitious men practiced a foolish liberality <note>A "foolish liberality" suggests the author's view that donors were overly generous or easily manipulated by religious fervor.</note> toward them.

<margin>Section 20. The vow of poverty made in the age of Jerome.</margin>

A vow of voluntary poverty was already taken by **Paula**, a Roman woman <note>Saint Paula of Rome (347–404 AD), a wealthy widow and disciple of Saint Jerome who distributed her riches to the poor and founded monasteries in Bethlehem.</note>, according to **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the Church Father best known for translating the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note> in Book 3, Epistle 8: *for she called God to witness that she had this wish: to die as a beggar herself, to not leave a single coin to her daughter, and to be wrapped in someone else’s shroud at her funeral. — Finally, she achieved what she desired, and left her daughter in great debt* <note>original: "grandi ære alieno"; literally "a great amount of someone else's copper," the Roman term for debt.</note>, *which, still owing it to this day, she trusts she will repay not by her own strength, but through the mercy of Christ.* Jerome himself frequently praises those who renounce their goods as perfect.

...and many there are who most happily enjoy this heavenly way of life. Furthermore, Siricius, who was elevated to the Roman See in the year 385 AD, censured both Monks and Virgins who did not keep their vow, as well as clerics or Ministers who did not abstain from relations with their spouses, in a stern Letter sent to Himerius, Bishop of Tarragona. <margin>§. 27. The fruits of the office.</margin>
This duty was enjoined upon our knights by the Patriarch and other bishops—as **William of Tyre** <note>William of Tyre (c. 1130–1186), a historian and Archbishop who wrote the most important contemporary account of the Kingdom of Jerusalem.</note> testifies—for the purpose of attaining *remission of sins* (see section 13). This can be understood partly as the pardon of punishments imposed by the Church, and partly as the appeasing of divine wrath, which weighs heavily upon crimes.

<margin>§. 28. Canonical satisfaction.</margin>
For the Church was accustomed, in order to restrain the license to sin, to impose defined penalties according to the **canons** <note>The laws and regulations governing the Church.</note> upon those who had fallen into crimes. Whoever endured these penalties made **satisfaction** <note>term: <term>satisfactio</term> (the performance of a penance to demonstrate repentance and repair the spiritual damage caused by sin)</note> to the Church and obtained forgiveness for the sin. Hence **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), an influential theologian whose writings shaped the medieval understanding of penance and grace.</note> in his work *To Laurence*, chapter 65.

<page-num>34</page-num>
# HISTORY
<meta>catchword: vibus- (part of "levibus," meaning minor or light sins)</meta>

*...therefore, for the purpose of restraining such an execrable presumption, it is ordered by the holy Council that penance be granted according to the form of the ancient canons; that is, that the priest should first cause the one who repents of his deed to be suspended from **communion** <note>term: <term>communion</term> (the participation in the Eucharist, the central act of Christian worship, from which sinners were temporarily excluded as a discipline)</note>, and to return frequently for the **imposition of hands** <note>original: "manus impositionem"; a ritual gesture where a priest places his hands on a person to signify the imparting of a blessing or the formal reception of a penitent back into the community.</note> among the remaining penitents. Once the time of **satisfaction** <note>term: <term>satisfaction</term> (the performance of specific acts, such as prayers or fasting, to demonstrate the sincerity of one's repentance and to "repay" the spiritual debt caused by sin)</note> is completed, as the priest’s discretion <note>original: "sacerdotalis contemplatio"</note> shall have approved, he may restore him to communion.*

<margin>§ 29. Means of obtaining the remission of sins according to the Ancients.</margin>

Next, the power of expiating sins and averting the punishments decreed in the court of God himself was attributed to prayers, fastings, and almsgivings—indeed, even to religious pilgrimages, the defense of pilgrims from bandits <note>This is a vital historical link to the Knights Templar, who were originally established specifically to provide this "work of penance" by protecting travelers to the Holy Land.</note>, and other works that are troublesome or joined with danger. **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential theologians in Western Christianity.</note> in his *Handbook to Laurentius*, chapter 70: *“God is to be appeased for past sins through almsgiving.”* And in chapter 72: *“There are many kinds of alms which, when we perform them, help us so that our sins may be forgiven.”* Concerning prayers, in chapter 71: *“Regarding daily light [offenses]...”*

<page-num>35</page-num>
-> [OF THE] TEMPLARS <-
C 2
<meta>catchword: triar- (completes the word "Patriarcha")</meta>

...and for light sins <note>original: "levibusque peccatis"; the text completes the word "levibus" from the previous page</note>, without which this life cannot be led, the daily prayer of the faithful provides **satisfaction**. <note>The author is citing a traditional view that while grave sins required public penance, minor daily sins were "satisfied" through regular prayer like the Lord’s Prayer.</note> Jerome <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), a Church Father best known for translating the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note>, in Book 1, Epistle 25, says: *“For my misdeeds I have moved to that wilderness which separates Syria from the border of the Barbarian lands.”* <note>original: "Pro facinoribus meis ad eam solitudinem commigravi, quæ Syriam juncto Barbariæ fine disterminat."</note> Indeed, Daillé <note>Jean Daillé (1594–1670), a prominent French Protestant theologian who argued that the practices of the early Church differed significantly from those of the Roman Catholic Church of his time.</note> argues brilliantly in Book 7 on punishments and satisfaction that the ancients felt very differently from the **Roman Church** about this subject, even if they used the same language. However, I cannot believe that the doctrine of the **justification** <note>term: <term>justification</term> (the theological concept of how a sinner is made righteous or "right" in the eyes of God)</note> of the sinner in the court of God was sufficiently understood by them, or clearly and distinctly passed down to the Christian people. Rather, I believe that for this very reason, this principal head of religion could easily be ignored in later centuries, corrupted by **superstition**, and entangled in errors, because after the death of the Apostles it was neither frequently enough nor fully and clearly set before the Church.

<margin>§ 30. The leader of the Templars.</margin>

To ensure the fulfillment of the office declared up to this point and the keeping of their vow, the Templars were bound especially by the **Patriar-** <note>The text cuts off here, continuing to "Patriarcha" (the Patriarch of Jerusalem) on the next page. The Patriarch was the highest-ranking bishop in Jerusalem and oversaw the initial formation of the Order.</note>

...[Pa]triarch of *Jerusalem*, to whom they submitted themselves from the beginning. <note>The text refers to the Knights Templar, who originally swore obedience to the Patriarch of Jerusalem.</note> "Patriarch" is a grand title, familiar to the **Seventy Translators** <note>The Septuagint (LXX), the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible into Greek.</note>, and by the power of its origin <note>original: "τῆς πατριᾶς ἄρχοντα" (tēs patrias archonta)</note> it signifies the prince or leader of a tribe or family. Thus Paul calls Abraham this in Hebrews 7:4. Stephen calls the twelve sons of Jacob this in Acts 7:8, 9. And Peter calls King David this in Acts 2:29. Later, when the Jews were dispersed far and wide after the destruction of Jerusalem, they appointed leading men over themselves in the East to maintain a certain appearance of unity, whom they likewise called Patriarchs, as **Du Fresne** <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange (1610–1688), a famous French scholar of Latin and Greek.</note> in his *Glossary*, **Beveridge** <note>William Beveridge (1637–1708), an English bishop and scholar of Church law.</note> in his *Annotations on the Canons of the Council of Nicaea*, and **Suicer** <note>Johann Caspar Suicer (1619–1684), author of a major dictionary of Greek ecclesiastical terms.</note> in his *Ecclesiastical Thesaurus* point out. But around the year 420, they were forbidden by the laws of the Emperors from having Patriarchs. From the Synagogue, the title passed into the **Christian Church**, and at first, bishops were referred to by this name without distinction, as the illustrious **Suicer** shows from **Nazianzen** <note>Saint Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390 AD).</note> and **Gregory of Nyssa** <note>c. 335–395 AD; both were influential early Christian theologians.</note>: but afterwards, Patriarchs were

...were named <note>original: "nominati"</note> **the leaders of each diocese**, <note>original Greek: "οἱ ἑκάστης διοικήσεως ἔξαρχοι" (hoi hekastēs dioikēseōs exarchoi). Learned men trace the origin of this institution to the **Council of Constantinople**, assembled in the year 381 against **Macedonius** <note>Macedonius I was a Bishop of Constantinople whose theological views on the Holy Spirit were condemned at this council.</note>. The Latin translation provided in the text is: "Episcopi ultra diœcesin in Ecclesias extra suos terminos ne accedant, nec Ecclesias confundant &c."</note>

However, the Council of Constantinople simply repeated a decree of the **Synod of Nicaea** <note>The First Council of Nicaea (325 AD), the first ecumenical council of the Christian Church.</note> by this canon, and that Council, in its 6th canon, commands that **"ancient customs should prevail."** <note>original Greek: "τὰ ἀρχεῖα ἔθη κρατεῖν" (ta archeia ethē kratein). This canon protected the traditional authority of major sees like Alexandria, Rome, and Antioch.</note> And so, from the first...

# HISTORY

<page-num>38</page-num>

...from the very earliest age of the Christian Church, just as the status <note>original: "dignitas"</note> of cities was unequal, and one city would raise its head above another, so too the **Bishops** of the more distinguished cities—especially the <term>metropolises</term> <note>A 'metropolis' was the mother-city or capital of a Roman province; in the Church, the bishop of such a city held authority over the surrounding province.</note>—surpassed the others in authority and power. Soon, however, these positions of honor gave rise to ambition; ambition led to disputes and quarrels, and one bishop would encroach upon the rights of another—whether those rights were acquired through an honorable title or usurped by force and fraud—as soon as an opportunity was found or created. To counter this evil, the **Council of Nicaea** <note>The First Council of Nicaea, held in 325 AD, was the first gathering of bishops from across the Christian world to establish unified doctrine and discipline.</note> enacted the sixth and seventh canons, which fixed specific boundaries for each jurisdiction.

On this point, the commentary written by **Balsamon** <note>Theodore Balsamon (c. 1140–1195) was the leading expert on Church law in the Byzantine Empire.</note> regarding the sixth canon is highly regarded: "The present canon and the seventh define the **four Patriarchs**: namely, those of **Rome**, **Alexandria**, **Antioch**, and **Jerusalem**; for the [Patriarch] of **Constantinople** will be addressed in other canons, and ancient customs are to be honored." <note>original Greek: "ὁ παρὼν κανὼν καὶ ὁ ζ'. διορίζονται τοὺς δ'. πατριάρχας. δηλαδὴ τὸν Ῥώμης, τὸν Ἀλεξανδρείας, τὸν Ἀντιοχείας, καὶ τὸν Ἱεροσολύμων, περὶ γὰρ τοῦ Κωνσταντινουπόλεως ἐν ἑτέροις κανόσι διαληφθήσεται, καὶ τὰ παλαιὰ ἔθη τιμᾶσθαι."</note> *The present and seventh canon establish four Patriarchs, namely the Roman, Alexandrian, Antiochian, and* <note>original Latin: "Præsens & ſeptimus canon ſtatuunt quatuor Patriarchas, vid. Romanum, Alexandrinum, Antiochenſem &"</note>

<meta>catchword: Hie- (likely referring to Hierosolymitanum / Jerusalem)</meta>

<summary>This page explains how the early Church hierarchy mirrorred the political importance of Roman cities, leading to power struggles that the Council of Nicaea attempted to resolve by codifying the jurisdictions of the primary Patriarchs.</summary>

<margin>§ 32. The Bishops of Jerusalem. And when they were made Patriarchs.</margin>

It is abundantly clear from **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), a historian often called the "Father of Church History."</note>, who in his *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 4, Chapter 5, reviews the succession of them, that the Church of **Jerusalem** was for a long time ruled by Bishops. And indeed, these Bishops of Jerusalem were subject to the **Metropolitan** <note>The high-ranking bishop of a provincial capital who held authority over other bishops in the region.</note>, the Bishop of **Caesarea** <note>The Roman administrative capital of Palestine; its bishop was the superior of the bishop in Jerusalem during this period.</note>. They were subject in such a way, however, that they held the first place after him among all the Bishops of Palestine, but without [administrative] power. Regarding this, the seventh <term>Canon</term> <note>A rule or decree made by a church council.</note> of the **Council of Nicaea** <note>The first ecumenical council of the Christian Church, held in 325 AD.</note> is explicit:

*Since custom and ancient tradition have prevailed, so that the Bishop in **Aelia** is to be honored, let him have the sequence of honor, while the proper dignity is preserved for the metropolis.* <note>original Greek: ἐπειδὴ συνήθεια κεκράτηκε, καὶ παράδοσις ἀρχαῖα, ὥστε τὸν ἐν Αἰλίᾳ ἐπίσκοπον τιμᾶσθαι, ἐχέτω τὴν ἀκολουθίαν τῆς τιμῆς, τῇ μητροπόλει σωζομένου τοῦ οἰκείου ἀξιώματος.</note> 

<note>Aelia Capitolina was the name given to Jerusalem by the Roman Emperor Hadrian in 135 AD after the Jewish-Roman wars; the Church continued to use this official name in legal documents for centuries.</note>

"Since custom and ancient tradition has obtained that he who is the Bishop in Aelia should be honored, let him have the consequence of honor"—or as the celebrated **Spanheim** <note>Friedrich Spanheim the Younger (1632–1694), a notable professor of theology and church history at Leiden.</note> more correctly translates it in the cited passage, "the second honor"—while the metropolis <note>original Latin: metropoli; the text cuts off here, continuing on the next page.</note>

# HISTORY

...while the proper dignity of the metropolis is preserved. <note>original: "poli propria dignitate servata." This concludes the quote from the Council of Nicaea (325 AD) which established that while Jerusalem deserved honor, the Bishop of Caesarea remained the administrative superior (Metropolitan).</note> But as time went on and Christians began to make frequent pilgrimages to **Jerusalem**, the Bishop of that place began to be held in greater honor than the Bishop of **Caesarea**. He dared to raise his head above the **Metropolitan** and seize the power over **Palestine** that was rightfully owed to the Bishop of Caesarea. **Juvenal** <note>Juvenal was the Bishop of Jerusalem from c. 422 to 458 AD; he was a highly ambitious prelate who sought to elevate his see to a patriarchate.</note> attempted this at the **Synod of Ephesus**, though he was restrained at that time by **Cyril of Alexandria**; however, he finally achieved his goal twenty years later at the **Council of Chalcedon** <note>The Council of Chalcedon in 451 AD finally granted Jerusalem the status of a Patriarchate, ranking it fifth in honor.</note>. For in the fifth session of that council, the two provinces of **Phoenicia** and **Arabia** were assigned to the Bishop of **Antioch**, while the three regions of Palestine—including **Caesarea**, the chief city of the first Palestine—were subjected to the Bishop of Jerusalem. One may read about how, in the following years, the **Patriarch of Jerusalem** tore away numerous churches from the **See of Antioch** in the works of **William of Tyre** <note>William of Tyre (c. 1130–1186) was a medieval historian and Archbishop; he is a primary source for the history of the Crusader states.</note>, who was a witness to the dispute, in Book 14, Chapter 11 and those following. Compare also **Marino Sanuto Torsello**, Part 7, Chapter 2, and **James of Vitry’s** *History of Jerusalem*, Chapters 55 and 56.

### THE TEMPLARS

<margin>
*The institutions of the Templars.*

...[of the orde]rs under the authority of the Patriarch of Jerusalem, let it suffice to have said this: now we must see when that order began. **William of Tyre** <note>William of Tyre (c. 1130–1186) was the Archbishop of Tyre and the most important historian of the early Crusades.</note>, a writer who lived close to those times, and after him **James of Vitry** <note>James of Vitry (c. 1160–1240) was a theologian and historian who served as the Bishop of Acre.</note> and **Matthew Paris** <note>Matthew Paris (c. 1200–1259) was an English Benedictine monk and chronicler known for his *Chronica Majora*.</note>, trace the start to the year **1118 AD**. This was the year when **Baldwin II** succeeded **Baldwin I** in the Kingdom of Jerusalem; **John II** <note>original: "Calo Johannes." This is a Latinization of the Greek *Kaloïōannēs* (John the Good/Handsome), referring to the Byzantine Emperor John II Komnenos.</note> succeeded **Alexios** in the Empire of Constantinople; **Gelasius II** succeeded **Paschal [II]** in the Roman See; and **Gormond**—a Frenchman from the province of Amiens, called "Warmund" <note>original: "Guarimundus"</note> by **Génébrard** <note>Gilbert Génébrard (1535–1597) was a French Benedictine scholar and Hebraist.</note> in his Chronicle—succeeded **Arnulf** in the See of Jerusalem; while **Henry V** ruled Germany, **Louis VI** ruled France, and **Henry I** ruled England. From this evidence, the *Great Belgian Chronicle* is corrected, in which it is read that *the order began in the year of our Lord 1119, under Baldwin, the first King of Jerusalem of this name.* <note>The author is pointing out a chronological error in the Belgian Chronicle, which misidentified the specific King Baldwin and the founding year.</note> **Polydore Vergil** <note>Polydore Vergil (c. 1470–1555) was an Italian humanist scholar who spent much of his life in England and wrote a famous history of inventions and "firsts."</note> also fell into a double error in Book 7, Chapter 5 of his work *On the Discovery of Things*, writing: *The Templar knights, around the year*

# HISTORY

<meta>catchword: Canon-</meta>

[In the year] of salvation 1128, under Pope **Gelasius II**, as some writers record, they began to exist in Jerusalem. For indeed, their order did not finally begin in that aforementioned year, but was confirmed by the **Council of Troyes** <note>original: "Synodo Trecensi." The Council of Troyes (January 1129) was where the Templar Order received official papal recognition and their Latin Rule. The author notes a discrepancy in the year, likely due to different calendar start dates (the New Year often began in March).</note>, as we shall soon hear. Furthermore, Gelasius II did not hold the Roman See at that time, having passed away ten years earlier; it was rather **Honorius II**, the successor to **Calixtus II** (who had been appointed head of the Roman Church after the death of Gelasius). Why **Antoninus of Florence** <note>St. Antoninus (1389–1459) was an Archbishop of Florence and a significant historian of the Middle Ages.</note>, as cited by **Hospinian** <note>Rudolf Hospinian (1547–1626) was a Swiss scholar who wrote extensively on the history of religious orders.</note> and **Wolf** <note>Johannes Wolf (1537–1600) was a German historian known for his *Lectionum memorabilium et reconditarum centenarii XVI* (Sixteen Centuries of Memorable and Recondite Readings).</note> in his *Memorable Readings*, traces the origin of the Templars to the year 1123, I cannot say.

<margin>
§ 34.
*The Place.*
</margin>
The name itself indicates the place where they first lived. For because they had their dwelling in the royal palace next to the **Temple of the Lord**, they are called the *brothers of the knighthood of the temple*; these are the words of **William of Tyre**. Namely: *since they had neither a church nor a fixed residence, the King granted them a temporary dwelling in the palace, which he holds on the southern side next to the Temple of the Lord.* <note>The "Temple of the Lord" refers to the Dome of the Rock, which the Crusaders mistakenly identified as the original Temple of Solomon; the "palace" refers to the Al-Aqsa Mosque.</note>

<summary>This page addresses the chronological confusion regarding the founding of the Knights Templar, correcting authors who misidentified the reigning Pope and the exact year of the Order's recognition. It also establishes that the Templars' name originated from their first headquarters at the Temple Mount in Jerusalem.</summary>

-># OF THE TEMPLARS<-

The Canons of the **Temple of the Lord** <note>original: "Canonici... templi Domini." During the Crusader occupation of Jerusalem, the Dome of the Rock was converted into a Christian church served by a group of clergy known as Canons Regular.</note>, however, granted the plaza that they held around the aforementioned palace for the use of workshops, under certain specific conditions, according to Book 12, Chapter 7 <note>This likely refers to William of Tyre’s *Historia Rerum in Partibus Transmarinis Gestarum* (History of Deeds Done Beyond the Sea), the primary source for the history of the Crusader States.</note>. **Jacques de Vitry** <note>A 13th-century historian and bishop of Acre who wrote the *Historia Hierosolymitana*.</note> reports the same in chapter 65, and **Matthew Paris** <note>A famous English Benedictine monk and chronicler of the 13th century.</note> transcribed almost the exact words of [William of] Tyre. **Volaterranus** <note>Raffaele Maffei (1451–1522), a humanist scholar from Volterra.</note> relates in his 21st book that they obtained from the King and the Abbot of the Temple permission to live in a portion of the buildings of the temple itself, a detail which **Polydore Vergil** <note>An Italian humanist historian (c. 1470–1555) who spent much of his life in England.</note> retained from him in the cited passage, and which **Bernard** <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), the Cistercian abbot who was the primary patron and protector of the Templars.</note> confirms in his *Exhortation to the Knights*, chapter 5.

There is indeed (he says) *a temple in Jerusalem in which they live together, certainly unequal in structure to that ancient and most famous one of Solomon, but not inferior in glory. — Yet the face of this temple is also adorned, but with weapons, not gems; and instead of ancient golden crowns, the wall is covered with hanging shields; instead of candelabras, censers, and pitchers, the house is fortified on all sides with bridles, saddles, and spears.* Clearly, all these things demonstrate plainly,

<center>D</center>

In that year <note>This refers back to the year 132 AD mentioned on the previous page.</note> there appeared in *Jerusalem* a certain man named *Bencocab* <note>Bar Kokhba; the name is Hebrew for "Son of a Star."</note>, who led the *Jews* astray by claiming he had descended from heaven like a star to liberate them from *Roman* servitude. After capturing *Jerusalem*, they destroyed the *Jews* and utterly demolished the city. Nearby, a new city was constructed which they called *Aelia Capitolina* <note>original Latin: *Aelia Adriani*</note>, in which they settled foreigners. *Hadrian* also ordered the ears of the surviving *Jews* to be cut off and imposed a law upon them: that no one should gaze upon *Jerusalem* even from a distance. ...to Christians born of Jewish descent. This indeed benefited the Christian faith, because at that time almost everyone believed in Christ as God while still observing the Law. Surely this was arranged by the Lord's ordering, so that the servitude of the Law might be removed from the freedom of the faith and the Church.*

Therefore, **Omar ibn al-Khattab** wrote a contract concerning this matter and handed it to him. Indeed, when the **Romans** <note>The Byzantines, who referred to themselves as Romans.</note> had embraced the Christian religion and **Helena**, the mother of **Constantine**, was building churches in **Jerusalem**, the site of the **Rock** and its surroundings had been laid waste and were therefore abandoned. <note>Historical traditions suggest the Temple Mount had been used as a refuse site by the Byzantines to signal the displacement of Judaism before Omar cleared it.</note>

Moreover, they had cast dust upon the **Rock** <note>original: "Petræ." This refers to the Foundation Stone (*Even ha-Shetiya*), the central feature of the Temple Mount in Jerusalem.</note>, to such an extent that a great dung heap lay over it. For this reason, the **Romans** <note>The Byzantines, who identified as Romans (*Rhomaioi*).</note> held it in neglect; they neither showed it the honor that the **Israelites** were accustomed to give, nor did they build a church upon it, because it was said by our Lord Christ in the Holy Gospel: *Behold, your house shall be left to you desolate*; and also: *not one stone will be left here upon another, which shall not be torn down and destroyed.* <note>The author cites Matthew 23:38 and 24:2 to explain why Christians left the Temple Mount in ruins—to serve as a visible testament to the fulfillment of Jesus's prophecy regarding the destruction of the Temple.</note> **Sophronius**, the **Patriarch**, therefore took **Omar ibn al-Khattab** by the hand and led him to the dung heap. **Omar**, taking the hem of his garment, filled it with the dust and cast it into the **Valley of Gehenna** <note>original: "vallem gehennæ." This refers to the Hinnom Valley, south of the Old City of Jerusalem.</note>. Seeing that **Omar ibn al-Khattab** had carried the dust in the lap of his robe, the **Muslims** <note>original: "Mohammedani."</note> likewise, without delay, all carried it away in their own laps, as well as in their clothes, shields, baskets, and jars, until the place was cleansed and purified and the **Rock** appeared. — Eutychius, Annals, part 2, page 287.

**Vocabulary from this page:**
**Rock**, **Romans**, **Israelites**, **Sophronius the Patriarch**, **Omar ibn al-Khattab**, **Muslims**, **Eutychius** <note>Eutychius of Alexandria (877–940 AD), a Melkite Patriarch and historian whose "Annals" are a primary source for this account.</note>

<summary>This page describes Caliph Omar's cleansing of the Foundation Stone on the Temple Mount, which had been turned into a refuse site by the Byzantines to symbolize the desolation of the Jewish Temple. Following Omar's lead, his followers cleared the debris to reveal the Rock, marking the beginning of the site's Islamic history.</summary>

### HISTORY

...of men <note>The syllable "rum" completes the word "virorum" (of men) from the previous page.</note> in his conception, birth, upbringing, and office, it is no wonder that he was also clothed in a unique manner; nor is there reason for us to be surprised by it or to blindly imitate it. The attire <note>original: "Habitus"</note> of the Lord Jesus was entirely ordinary, consisting of a *tunic and a cloak*, as seen in John 19:23. The tunic was of the variety of "round" garments which, being closed all the way around the outer edge, had no corners. Regarding the cloak, **Tertullian** <note>An influential early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD).</note> notes: *The cloak is an outer garment, and is itself four-cornered*; for this reason, from the four corners of Christ’s cloak at the crucifixion, the soldiers were able to make four parts. Following the Master's example, the Apostles were to be dressed in nothing but a single tunic and cloak. *Do not possess two tunics*, as commanded in Matthew 10:10 (compare also Mark 6:9 and Acts 12:8). Indeed, the ancients were accustomed to wearing not just two, but sometimes three or even more tunics. All philosophers, except for the **Cynics** <note>A Greek school of philosophy known for rejecting luxury and social conventions in favor of a simple life.</note>, wore both an *inner and an outer* tunic—an <term>under-garment</term> <note>original Greek: ὑποδύτην (hypodytēn)</note> and an <term>over-garment</term> <note>original Greek: ἐπενδύτην (ependytēn)</note>—and over both of these, they wore a cloak. The Romans, too, were for a long time content with a single [tunic]...

<header>->OF THE TEMPLARS<-</header>

...tunic, but afterward they began to wear two, one of which they called a <term>subucula</term> <note>original: "subuculam"; a Roman term for a linen under-tunic or undershirt worn next to the skin.</note> and the other an <term>indusium</term> <note>original: "indusium"; a second tunic or shirt worn over the subucula.</note>, as **Salmasius** <note>Claude Saumaise (1588–1653), a French classical scholar famous for his massive editions of ancient texts.</note> observes in his notes on **Tertullian’s** <note>An early Christian author from Carthage.</note> work *On the Mantle*.

But Christ forbade his disciples from everything that pertained to decoration or luxury—which is especially cumbersome for a traveler—and for this reason, he permitted them the use of *sandals*, but not *shoes* (Matthew 10:10; Mark 6:9). Now, a <term>shoe</term> <note>original Greek: ὑπόδημα (hypodēma)</note> is a hollow footwear into which the foot is placed and by which it is entirely covered; whereas a <term>sandal</term> <note>original Greek: σανδάλιον (sandalion)</note> is a sole or slipper that only covers the sole of the foot, leaving the upper part exposed. Peter wore such sandals (Acts 12:8). 

The Lord Jesus warns that false prophets put on distinctive clothing made from sheepskins (Matthew 7:15). Yet the Hebrews, who suffered every misery at the hands of their enemies for the name of Christ, wandered about in *sheepskins and goatskins* (Hebrews 11:37), not because they chose such a style, but because their poverty demanded it.

...custom, and since they wished to present themselves as followers of this way in all things, just as they were distinguished by the title of "Philosophers," so also they loved to wear the **pallium** <note>original: "pallium"; a rectangular cloak draped over the shoulder, distinct from the rounded Roman toga.</note>, which was characteristic of Greek philosophers. This was a garment of sufficient modesty and of a dark or somber color, which the Greeks called a <term>tribon</term> <note>original Greek: τρίβωνα; a coarse, thin cloak worn by ascetics and philosophers to show their disdain for luxury.</note>, <term>tribonion</term> <note>original Greek: τριβώνιον; a diminutive form of the tribon.</note>, or <term>rakon</term> <note>original Greek: ῥάκον; literally a "rag" or "shred," emphasizing extreme poverty.</note>. **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), an early historian of the Christian Church.</note> testifies in his *Ecclesiastical History*, book 4, chapter 11, that **Justin Martyr** preached the divine word **in the habit of a philosopher** <note>original Greek: ἐν Φιλοσόφου σχήματι (en Philosophou schēmati)</note>. However, I believe that this wise man did not use that clothing so much to distinguish himself from other Christians, but rather because he wished to attract listeners who were still devoted to pagan rituals to his sermons. And **Tertullian** <note>An influential early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD).</note>, having become a presbyter or priest, cast off the toga and put on the *pallium*; for this, he was criticized by the people of Carthage, and in response, he published a book *On the Mantle*, which he calls the **priestly habit** <note>original: "habitum sacerdotalem"</note>, since Christian priests at that time made use of it. **Heraclas**, an Alexandrian presbyter and contemporary of **Origen**, when he entered the school of a master philosopher, having **previously [dressed] in common clothing** <note>original Greek: πρότερον κοινῇ ἐσθῆτι (proteron koinē esthēti); the sentence breaks here, and the Greek word for "clothing" concludes on the next page.</note>

He was so free from all vanity that often, among the vast crowds of such a great city [Constantinople], which was so famous, he would go about wearing **sandals made of hay** <note>original Greek: χορτίνων σανδαλίων; Latin: *sandaliis... ex fœno confectis*.</note>, as recorded by **Socrates** <note>Socrates Scholasticus (c. 380–439 AD), a church historian.</note> in Book 7, Chapter 36.

# HISTORY

<margin>The philosophical habit received into the Church.</margin> They wished to show themselves as devoted followers in all things, and just as they were distinguished by the title of **Philosophers**, so too did they love to wear the **pallium** <note>original: *pallium*; a simple rectangular cloak associated with intellectuals</note>, which was characteristic of Greek philosophers. This was a garment modest enough and of a dark color <note>original: *pullo*; a dusky or blackish-gray color associated with mourning or serious study</note>; the Greeks called it a *tribon*, *tribonion*, or *rhakion* <term>tribon</term> <note>original Greek: *τρίβωνα*, *τριβώνιον*, *ῥάκιον*; these terms all denote a "worn" or "coarse" cloak used by ascetics</note>. **Eusebius** testifies in his *Ecclesiastical History* (Book 4, Chapter 11) that **Justin Martyr** preached the divine word while **in the habit of a philosopher** <note>original Greek: *ἐν Φιλοσόφου σχήματι*; original Latin: *in habitu Philosophi*</note>. Although I believe that this wise man intended, by wearing that dress, not so much to distinguish himself from other Christians as to attract listeners who were still devoted to pagan rites to his assemblies.

And **Tertullian**, having become a presbyter or priest, cast aside the [Roman] **toga** and put on the **pallium**. For this reason, after being rebuked by the people of Carthage, he published a book *On the Pallium*, which he calls a **priestly habit** <term>habitus sacerdotalis</term>, since Christian priests used it at that time. **Heraclas**, a presbyter of Alexandria and a contemporary of **Origen**, when he devoted himself to the school of a master philosopher, was **previously [clad] in common clothing** <note>original Greek: *πρότερον κοινῇ ἐσθῆτι*</note>...

->### OF THE TEMPLARS<-

...used this dress; having stripped off the common one and taken up the philosophical habit, he has maintained it until now. <note>original Greek: σθῆτι χρώμενος, ἀποδυσάμενος, καὶ Φιλόσοφον ἀναλαβὼν σχῆμα, μέχρι τοῦ δεῦρο τηρεῖ</note> *Having rejected the common garment he used before, he assumed the philosophical habit, and afterward preserved it*, as written in **Eusebius**, Book 6, Chapter 19. 

But contrary to the custom of other **ascetics** <note>original Greek: ἀσκητῶν; those practicing strict self-discipline for religious reasons</note>, **Sylvanus**, though he strived to live as a Christian with great precision and followed the ascetic life, did not choose to wear the **coarse cloak** <term>tribon</term> <note>original Greek: τρίβωνα; a simple, rough cloak often worn by philosophers and monks to show their rejection of luxury</note>. *Endeavoring to diligently cultivate the Christian religion and having pursued the monastic life, he refused to wear the pallium.* <note>The Latin translation in the source uses "pallium" to translate the Greek "tribon," highlighting how these terms for simple cloaks were often used interchangeably in a religious context.</note> He was so free from all pride that he would frequently go about in the great multitude of that city [Constantinople], which was so famous, wearing **sandals made of straw**. <note>original Greek: ἀπὸ χορτίνων σανδαλίων; original Latin: sandaliis indutus ex fœno confectis</note> As recorded by **Socrates [Scholasticus]**, Book 7, Chapter 36.

<margin>§. 49. Alexander Severus’s edict on the distinction of garments.</margin> 
In the Republic, **Alexander Severus** <note>Roman Emperor from 222 to 235 AD, known for his administrative reforms</note> intended to assign a specific type of clothing to all offices and all ranks, so that they might be distinguished by their dress...

# HISTORIA

But this plan was displeasing to **Ulpian** and **Paulus** <note>two of the most influential Roman jurists and advisors to the Emperor</note>, who argued that there would be a great many brawls if people were so easily exposed to insults <note>The jurists feared that identifying slaves by dress would invite public abuse and lead to social unrest</note>. Then he [the Emperor] decided it was sufficient for **Roman Knights** to be distinguished from **Senators** by the quality of the **stripe** <term>clavus</term> <note>original Latin: clavi qualitate; the *clavus* was a purple band on the tunic—wide for senators and narrow for knights</note>. <margin>§. 50. The garment of Paul the first Hermit.</margin>
In **Egypt**, the palm tree provided both food and clothing to **Paul**, the founder of the solitary life; yet, as he was dying, he said to **Anthony**: *“Bring the cloak <note>original Latin: pallium</note> which Bishop Athanasius gave to you, to wrap my poor little body.”* Blessed Paul made this request, not because he was greatly concerned whether his corpse would rot while covered or naked—seeing as he, for such a long span of time, had clothed <note>original Latin: vestierentur</note> himself in woven palm leaves—

<margin>§. 51. The *Colobium* of Monks; the *Dalmatica* of Priests.</margin>
Before the times of **Constantine the Great** <note>Roman Emperor (reigned 306–337 AD) who issued the Edict of Milan, ending the persecution of Christians</note>, all in general, both Monks and Clerics, were content with a humbler habit. For an inner garment they wore the *colobium* <term>colobium</term> <note>original: *colobio*; a simple, sleeveless or short-sleeved tunic</note>, which was a tunic without sleeves, or certainly with shorter sleeves reaching barely to the elbow, named from the Greek *kolobos* <term>kolobos</term> <note>original Greek: κολοβὸς; meaning "docked," "curtailed," or "shortened"</note>; for an outer garment, they wore the *pallium* <term>pallium</term> <note>a traditional Greek-style cloak</note>. But once wealth was granted to the Church, its ministers soon preferred to be dressed more elegantly. In place of the *colobium* succeeded the *Dalmatica* <term>dalmatic</term> <note>original: *Dalmatica*; a long-sleeved, wide-bodied tunic</note>, introduced—as is commonly held—by **Sylvester I** <note>Pope from 314 to 335 AD during the reign of Constantine</note>. This was a type of sleeved tunic first discovered in **Dalmatia**; at first it was considered shameful, used by the luxurious and delicate, but later it became the specific garment of Priests, which they wore in white with purple stripes <term>clavi</term> <note>original: *clavis*; vertical decorative bands that traditionally denoted rank in Roman society</note>.

And this—whether one considers it the luxury, the ornament, or the proper decorum of sacred vestments, as different people interpreted it differently—was the catalyst for a stricter ***monastic and ascetic way of life*** <note>original Greek: μοναστικῆς & ἀσκητικῆς πολιτείας</note>. ...acted in many ways contrary to ecclesiastical standards. For he forbade marriages to take place and taught that one must abstain from certain foods. Consequently, he separated some who had contracted marriages from their wedlock: and he persuaded those who detested the Churches to hold communion in their own houses. He drew slaves away from their masters under a pretense of piety: he himself wore the habit of a philosopher, and compelled his followers to use a foreign kind of clothing.*

Socrates, Book 2, Chapter 33. Compare with Sozomen, Book 3, Chapter 13. The Canon of the Council of Gangra <note>A regional church council held to address the excesses of the Eustathian movement</note> enacted against Eustathius and his disciples, being the 12th in order, reads as follows:

<note>original Greek: Εἴ τις ἀνδρῶν διὰ νομιζομένην ἄσκησιν περιβολαίῳ χρῆται, καὶ ὡς ἂν ἐκ τούτου τὴν δικαιοσύνην ἔχων, καταψηφίσοιτο τῶν μετ’ εὐλαβείας τοὺς βήρους φορούντων, καὶ τῇ ἀλλῇ κοινῇ, καὶ ἐν συνηθείᾳ οὔσῃ ἐσθῆτι κεχρημένων, ἀνάθεμα ἔστω.</note>

*“If any man, because of a supposed ascetic practice, uses a mantle <note>original: περιβολαίῳ (peribolaio), referring to the philosopher's cloak or pallium</note>, and as if deriving righteousness from it, should condemn those who with piety wear the byrrus <note>original: βήρους (berous)</note> and make use of other common and customary clothing, let him be anathema <note>A formal curse or excommunication by the church</note>.”*

<margin>
§. 53.
What kind of garment is the Byrrus?
</margin>

From this decree it is clearly evident that at the time of the Council of Gangra—which Zonaras <note>Joannes Zonaras, a 12th-century historian and commentator on church law</note> writes was convened after the first Council of Nicaea—the mantle and the "philosopher's habit" <note>original: σχῆμα Φιλοσόφου (schema philosophou)</note> were considered "strange dress" <note>original: ξενὸν ἀμφίεσμα (xenon amphiesma)</note>, as it is expressly called in the Synodical Letter prefixed to the Canons; or, in the words of Sozomen, "strange and unusual clothing" <note>original: ξενὴν καὶ ἀήθεα ἐσθῆτα</note>. Furthermore, the *berri* or *byrri* <term>byrrus</term> <note>A thick, woolen outer cloak, often hooded, which was standard daily wear in the late Roman Empire before becoming a specific clerical garment</note> were held in opposition to this [ascetic] habit...

It highlights the early Church's resistance to radical ascetic dress that deviated from established social customs.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological and historical investigation into the "byrrus," the garment defended by the Council of Gangra against radical ascetics. The previous page established that the byrrus was the standard, pious dress for Christians of the time. This page transitions into the etymology of the word and its evolution from a functional Roman rain-cloak to a prestigious silk vestment used by high-ranking officials and the clergy.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>OF THE TEMPLARS.</header>

<sig>E 5</sig>

...garment <note>Completing the word "habitum" from the previous page</note> accepted by common usage. The name of this clothing varies: for at times it is called *berus*, and at other times *birrus*. Salmasius <note>Claude Saumaise (1588–1653), a renowned French classical scholar</note> prefers to write it as *byrrus*, deriving it from the Greek *pyrrhos* <note>original Greek: πυῤῥὸς</note>, because it was of a fiery or reddish color; indeed, according to the testimony of Festus <note>Sextus Pompeius Festus, a 2nd-century Roman grammarian</note>, the word *burrus* meant "red" among the ancient Latins. The garment itself differed in color and material according to the status and dignity of the wearers. It was first used by the Romans to ward off cold and rain, especially when they were traveling, and was called a *lacerna* or a *penula* <note>Types of heavy Roman outer cloaks or overcoats</note>. This is the *phelone* <note>original Greek: Φελόνη</note> which Paul desired Timothy to bring to him before winter, as seen in 2 Timothy 4:13, 21. Indeed, it is likely that *byrri* were originally made from a cheaper and thicker cloth, as they were intended to repel cold and rain. However, after they began to be worn by Senators in the Republic and by Clergy in the Church, they were woven from a finer and more precious material; hence Balsamon and Zonaras <note>12th-century Byzantine commentators on church law</note> interpret them as "silk fabrics" <note>original Greek: σηρικὰ ὑφάσματα (serika hyphasmata)</note>. On this matter, learned men commonly cite the saying of Augustine from his 50th sermon, *On Various Subjects*: "Let there be offered to me..."

<summary>This page explores the etymology and history of the "birrus," a hooded cloak that evolved from a coarse Roman traveler's rain-cape into a refined silk vestment worn by senators and high-ranking clergy.</summary>

(The holy Father says) *for example, a precious byrrus <note>a cloak or mantle, often made of wool or silk</note> perhaps suits a Bishop, although it does not suit Augustine, that is, a poor man born of poor parents:* regarding this passage, it is useful to observe in passing that the silk byrrus was a garment specific to Bishops, and was not common to priests or deacons. Certainly, monks of that age never dressed themselves in a byrrus. Cassian <note>John Cassian (c. 360–435), an influential monk and theologian</note> in Book 1 of *On the Institutes of Monks*, Chapter 7, states: *They avoid both the cost and the vanity of byrri:* and the *Rule of Isidore* <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), a scholar and bishop</note>, Chapter 13: *It is not proper for a monk to be dressed in linen; it is not lawful to use the <term>orarium</term>, the <term>birri</term>, or the <term>planetae</term>.* Among writers of a later age, the <term>orarium</term> <note>a term for a handkerchief or a liturgical stole; the text notes it was used to wipe the mouth or cover the face</note> is sometimes a cloth for wiping the mouth, and sometimes a band that covers the face; and the <term>planeta</term> <note>a large, circular traveling cloak that eventually became the liturgical chasuble</note> is a priestly garment placed over all other clothes, so called because its borders "wander" or flow freely <note>The author derives "planeta" from the Greek word for "wanderer," describing how the large cloak moves around the wearer</note>.

<margin>Section 56. Luxury in the clothing of Clerics.</margin>
In the following centuries, luxury and pomp grew in the clothing of Clerics, which many Councils attempted to restrain. For instance, the Fourth Council of Carthage <note>a significant African church council</note>, which Cabassutius <note>Jean Cabassut (1604–1685), a French priest and historian of canon law</note> relates to the year of Christ 398, says in Canon 45: *A Cleric should demonstrate his profession in both his attire and his gait, and therefore should not seek elegance in either his clothes or his shoes.* The Council of Narbonne <note>a regional council in Gaul, modern France</note> defined the habit in the year 589 in Canon 1: *It is established by rule that no Cleric shall wear purple garments, which pertain to worldly boasting rather than religious dignity, so that just as there is devotion in the mind, so also it may be shown in the body.* The Council of Trullo <note>also called the Quinisext Council, held in Constantinople to establish disciplinary canons</note>, assembled in the year 692, stated in Canon 27: *No one listed among the clergy shall dress in unsuitable clothing, whether living in the city or walking on the road, but they shall use the robes already assigned to those who are enrolled in the clergy.* <note>original Greek: Μηδεὶς τῶν ἐν κλήρῳ καταλεγομένων ἀνοίκειον ἐσθῆτα ἀμφιεννύσθω, μήτε ἐν πόλει διάγων, μήτε ἐν ὁδῷ βαδίζων, ἀλλὰ στολαῖς κεχρήσθω ταῖς ἤδη τοῖς ἐν κλήρῳ καταλεγομένοις ἀπονενεμημέναις.</note>

<summary>This page documents the historical shift from simple monastic dress to increasingly luxurious clerical clothing, citing various church councils from the 4th to 7th centuries that issued canons to restrict vanity and enforce modest, standardized attire.</summary>
