No one among those who are enrolled in the registry of the Clergy should wear a garment that is not suitable for him, whether he is staying in the city or traveling on the road; rather, he should use the garments that were previously assigned to those who have been enrolled in the clergy. But if anyone should do such a thing, let him be segregated for one week.* The same Fathers of the Trullan Council <note>The Quinisext Council of 692, which met in the "Trullo" or domed hall of the imperial palace to complete the work of previous ecumenical councils</note> stripped Macarius <note>Macarius I, the Patriarch of Antioch, who was deposed for his theological views</note> and his Monothelite disciples <note>adherents of Monothelitism, the belief that Christ had two natures but only one will, which was declared heretical</note> in the eighth session of their *episcopal pallium <note>a liturgical vestment symbolic of papal or archiepiscopal authority</note>, namely the white tunic.* The Second Council of Nicaea <note>the seventh ecumenical council, held in 787 AD, primarily known for resolving the Iconoclast controversy</note> in the year 787, in Canon 16: *All luxury and bodily adornment are foreign to the priestly rank and status. Therefore, those who adorn themselves with bright and conspicuous garments should be corrected; if they persist, they should be subjected to penance; likewise also those who apply perfumes.* <note>original Greek: "Πᾶσα βλακεία καὶ κόσμησις σωματικὴ..."</note> *All luxury and bodily adornment is foreign to the priestly order and state. Therefore, Bishops or Clerics who [adorn] themselves...*

<margin>§ 57. The diversity and holiness of the garments of Monks.</margin>

Yet as for the habit <note>the distinctive clothing worn by members of a religious order</note> of the Monks, according to the variety of their orders <note>original Latin: "familiarum," literally families or houses</note>, which sprouted in great numbers especially in the West, various colors distinguished them, unique forms marked them out, and human effort defined them—not so badly at the beginning, but later recklessly and ineptly, until finally the superstition of unhappy times placed them among sacred things endowed with I know not what divine power. The **Rule of Augustine**, chapter 6, as cited in **Hugh of Saint Victor**: *Let your habit not be conspicuous, nor should you strive to please by your clothes, but by your conduct.* **Benedict** <note>Saint Benedict of Nursia (c. 480–547 AD), the founder of Western monasticism and author of the "Rule of Saint Benedict"</note>, who emerged as the father of Western Monks around the beginning of the 6th century, writes in Chapter 55 of his **Rule**: *Clothing should be given to the brothers according to the nature of the places where they live or the climate; for in cold regions more is needed, but in warm ones, less. This consideration, therefore, lies with the Abbot. We believe that for moderate locations, it suffices*

# TEMPLAR MATTERS <note>original Latin: "TEMPLARIORUM"</note>

*...to each monk a cowl and a tunic: a shaggy cowl in winter, but in summer one that is plain or old; also a scapular for work. For footwear, socks and boots. Monks should not complain about the color or coarseness of these things, but use whatever can be found in the province where they live, or what can be purchased more cheaply. The Abbot, however, should take care of the sizing, so that the garments are not too short for those wearing them, but well-fitted.* 

A scapular <term>scapulare: a garment suspended from the shoulders</term> is a short, small cloak covering only the head and shoulders, used by Monks instead of a cowl when they were performing manual labor. "Pedules" <note>literally "foot-things"</note> are coverings for the feet, elsewhere called "tibialia" <note>leggings or stockings</note>. 

Furthermore, **Polydore Vergil** <note>an Italian humanist scholar (c. 1470–1555) who wrote a famous history of "firsts" or inventions</note> describes the first habit of the **Benedictines** in *On the Discovery of Things*, Book 7, Chapter 2. 

In the year 530, the *Apostolic Order* <note>original Latin: "ordo Apostolicus"</note> of Monks arose in Britain. Their clothing consisted of a sheepskin cloak <term>melote: a rough garment made of sheep or goat skin, associated with ascetics</term> made from goat hides, a narrow cowl, and a white stole. **The Council in Trullo** <note>original Latin: "Synodus Trullana," a 7th-century church council that established many rules for Eastern Orthodox practice</note>, Canon 42.

In the year 1031, *Dominic <note>likely Saint Dominic Loricatus (c. 995–1060), an ascetic monk</note>, a performer of wonderful works and the founder of many monasteries, died in Campania at nearly eighty years of age. Peter Damian <note>a leading reformer and theologian of the 11th century</note> calls him "the armored one" <note>original: "loricatum"</note>, because he used a coat of mail <term>lorica: typically a soldier's metal armor, here worn as a shirt of penance</term> to torment his flesh beneath the roughness of his inner clothing.* 

Regarding Peter the Hermit <note>a key figure in the First Crusade</note>, whom we also mentioned above in section 50, Guibert, the Abbot of the *History of Jerusalem* <note>Guibert of Nogent, author of "Gesta Dei per Francos"</note>, Book 2, chapter 8, writes: *Whatever he did or spoke seemed as if it were something semi-divine, especially since even the hairs from his mule were snatched up as relics—though we report this not as truth, but as a reflection of the common people's love for novelty. He wore a woolen tunic against his bare skin, with a hood over both garments reaching to his ankles, and a cloak over the top; he wore no breeches <note>original: "brachis"</note> at all, and went barefoot.* 

In this passage, "cloak" <note>original: "byrrus"</note> signifies a mantle suited for traveling. 

Regarding Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux, the Cistercian reformer</note>, the aforementioned Sigonius <note>Carlo Sigonio</note> in Book 11 of *On the Kingdom of Italy* at the year 1134, says: *The citizens were eager to address him and kiss his feet; indeed, they even [plucked] hairs from his garments*

<margin>§. 59. The red cross on the cloaks of the Templars.</margin>

From the time of Eugenius III—who in the year 1145 of the common era was made Roman Pope, having previously been a Cistercian monk and a disciple of Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux, who was instrumental in the founding of the Templars</note>—the Templars *began to sew crosses of red cloth onto their mantles, so that they might be more prominent among others, both the knights and their lower-ranking brothers, who are called sergeants* <term>servientes: the non-noble members of the military order who performed military and administrative support</term>, according to William of Tyre <note>the primary historian of the early Crusades</note>, book 12, chapter 7. <margin>§. 60. The various uses of the cross in the early Church.</margin>

And here, a wide field of discussion presents itself to us regarding the various *uses* of the Cross in the early Church <note>original: "Veteri Ecclesia"</note>, and the ancient origin of those uses: however, our speech will be confined within modest limits, as it will present only the principal part of this most extensive subject. And indeed, the *use* can be referred to roughly three main headings: for either the ancient Christians, by making the sign of the cross on their foreheads, commended their goings out and comings in, or their other actions, to God and Christ the Savior <note>original: "Christo Servatori"</note> who was nailed to the cross; or they wore crosses manufactured from various materials, hung upon their clothes, or even carried them in their hands; or finally, crosses of larger size made of wood or sto- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; "lapi-" likely completes as "lapide," meaning stone</note>

<margin>§. 61. The sign of the cross on the forehead.</margin>
Tertullian <note>Tertullian (c. 155–220 AD) was an influential early Christian author from Carthage; he is often called the "father of Latin theology."</note> mentions the custom of marking the forehead with a cross in very clear words in a well-known passage from his book *On the Soldier's Crown*: *“At every step and movement, at every coming in and going out, when putting on clothes and shoes, at the baths, at the tables, at the lighting of lamps, at our beds, at our seats, in whatever conversation or activity we are engaged, we wear away our foreheads with the sign <note>original: "frontem signaculo terimus." This suggests the frequent, repetitive nature of the gesture.</note>. If you should demand a law from the Scriptures for these and other such practices, no tradition of law will be offered to you; custom is the creator, tradition is the strengthener, and faith is the observer of them.”* Lactantius, in his *Divine Institutes*, book 4, chapter 26 <note>Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD) was a scholar and advisor to Emperor Constantine the Great.</note> says: *“Christ was a white lamb without blemish, that is, innocent and just and holy, who, having been sacrificed by those same Jews, is salvation to all who have written the sign of blood—that is, the cross upon which He shed His blood—upon their foreheads.”* Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD) was a priest and scholar best known for translating the Bible into Latin, known as the Vulgate.</note>, book 2, Epistle 19.

*“At every act, at every step, let the hand trace the cross.”* <note>original: "manus pingat crucem." This implies a repetitive, ritualistic motion in daily life.</note> It is clear from Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential theologians in the history of the Church.</note> that from a very tender age, infants—or parents on behalf of their infants—observed this custom. He says in his *Confessions*, book 1, chapter 11: *“I was already being signed with the sign of His cross, and I was already being seasoned with His salt <note>In the early Church, salt was given to catechumens (those preparing for baptism) as a symbol of wisdom and preservation from corruption.</note> even from my mother’s womb.”*

<margin>§ 62. A false reason for this sign.</margin>
There are learned men who believe that the early Christians used the sign of the cross for the sole purpose of clearly distinguishing themselves from the pagans among whom they lived, and to publicly testify to the glory they placed in the cross of Christ. But, if I may speak with all due respect to them, they are significantly mistaken in this. Indeed, before the time of Constantine the Great <note>The Roman Emperor (reigned 306–337 AD) who legalized Christianity through the Edict of Milan.</note>, when the Church finally received peace, Christians did not sign their foreheads with the cross openly, but in secret, so that those who were strangers to the doctrine of Christ would not see it. Tertullian <note>A prolific early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD).</note> makes this certain for me in book 2 of his work *To His Wife*. He says: *“Do not cast your pearls before swine, lest they trample them underfoot, and turning back they also [tear] you...”* <note>The text ends mid-sentence, quoting Matthew 7:6 to justify the "Discipline of the Secret," where Christians hid their rituals from outsiders to avoid mockery or persecution.</note>

<meta>catchword: que [likely leading into the Latin "querant" or "queant"]</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the early Christian practice of the sign of the cross, specifically its use in infancy as mentioned by Augustine, and refutes the idea that it was used as a public badge before the reign of Constantine.</summary>
<keywords>Augustine, sign of the cross, Constantine the Great, Tertullian, Discipline of the Secret, infant baptism, early church customs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of early Christian secrecy regarding the sign of the cross, specifically citing Tertullian's advice to his wife about the difficulties of being married to a pagan. <page-num>89</page-num>

->F 5<-

...lest they trample them. <note>original: "que evertant." This completes the sentence from the previous page, referencing Matthew 7:6 regarding "pearls before swine."</note> *“Your pearls are the daily marks of your conduct. The more you take care to hide them, the more suspicious you will make them, and the more they must be guarded against pagan curiosity. Will you be hidden when you sign your bed or your own little body?”* From these words of Tertullian, I conclude that there were two reasons why Christians, in the presence of pagans, abstained from other rites as well as the custom of signing the forehead with the cross: one, because they judged profane men unworthy of witnessing our sacred mysteries; the other, because they did not wish to rashly betray themselves to Christ’s enemies and incur their violent anger.

In the same work cited above, when he <note>Tertullian.</note> recounts the privileges of a marriage entered into by two Christians, he says: *“Almsgiving without torment, sacrifices without hesitation. Daily diligence without hindrance. A signing that is not done in secret.”* <note>original: "Non furtiva signatio." In a Christian marriage, the sign of the cross could be performed openly and joyfully between spouses, rather than hidden in fear.</note> For, to speak from the perspective of Beatus Rhenanus <note>Beatus Rhenanus (1485–1547) was a prominent German humanist and scholar who edited the works of Tertullian.</note>, a Christian woman did not dare to sign herself while her pagan husband was watching, but she did this secretly—

<sig>C. I.</sig>

—doing so. <note>This continues the thought from the previous page: a Christian woman did things secretly when her pagan husband was watching.</note> For the same reason, they did not utter the name of Christ when a pagan was listening. For Tertullian continues: *“There is no fearful rejoicing, no silent blessing. Between the two of them, Psalms and hymns echo, and they mutually challenge each other to see who can sing better to their God.”* <note>original: "Non trepida gratulatio, non muta benedictio. Sonant inter duos Psalmi & hymni, & mutuo provocant, quis melius Deo suo canet." From Tertullian’s *To His Wife* (Ad Uxorem), describing the joys of a marriage where both spouses are Christian.</note> However, I do not deny that sometimes the Martyrs testified to their constancy in the profession of the Gospel by making the sign of the cross in some way amidst their torments. For Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), an early Christian historian.</note>, in Book 8, chapter 7 of his *Ecclesiastical History*, mentions a youth who suffered a dreadful death for the name of Christ in Phoenicia under Diocletian <note>Roman Emperor who led the final Great Persecution of Christians (r. 284–305 AD).</note>, **and who stretched out his hands across in the manner of a cross.** <note>original: "καὶ τὰς μὲν χεῖρας ἐφαπλῶντος εἰς σταυροῦ τύπον" / "& manus in crucis modum è transverso expandentis."</note> I also concede that after the time of Constantine <note>The first Roman Emperor to convert to Christianity, ending the era of persecution.</note>, Christians used the sign of the cross more freely and almost as an insult in the presence of the Pagans, as they no longer feared any harm from them. And to this belongs a passage from the 13th Catechesis of Cyril of Jerusalem <note>A distinguished theologian and Bishop of Jerusalem (c. 313–386 AD).</note>, cited by Larrogius <note>Matthieu de Larroque (1619–1684), a French Protestant scholar known for his works on ecclesiastical history.</note> in part 3.

<summary>This page discusses the evolution of the sign of the cross from a secret ritual performed by persecuted Christians to a public act of defiance and faith following the reign of Constantine. It references historical accounts from Tertullian and Eusebius to illustrate how the practice was adapted depending on the safety of the believers.</summary>

<header>OF THE TEMPLARS.</header>

...more openly. <note>This continues the thought from the previous page regarding the increasing public use of the sign of the cross.</note> As cited in Chapter 1 of the *History of the Eucharist*: *“Set up a trophy of the faith of the cross against those who contradict you. For when you face a dispute with unbelievers regarding the Lord and the cross, first form the sign with your hand, and the opponent will fall silent.”* <note>original: "Trophæum statuas fidem crucis adversus contradicentes. Cum enim de Domino cruceque contra infideles quæstio tibi erit, prius statue manu tuâ signum, & obmutescet contradicens."</note>

<margin>§. 63. Truth, and the remembrance of Christ.</margin>
Therefore, to present other and more certain facts regarding the use of the sign of the cross, we state first that the early Christians, through that sign, recalled to their memory Jesus Christ hanging upon the cross. This was also the view held by Leo Allatius <note>Leo Allatius (1586–1669) was a Greek scholar and theologian who worked to reconcile the Eastern and Western Churches.</note> in his work *On the Perpetual Agreement of the Western and Eastern Church*, Book 3, Chapter 18, Section 14. He writes: *“The ancients were accustomed, not only when they began a task but also as they finished it, to seal it with the sign of the cross as a remarkable example of piety. It is a most excellent practice to always have the token <term>tessera<gloss>a token or sign used as a password or identifying mark</gloss></term> of our redemption before our eyes, at both the start and the conclusion of our endeavors.”* This usage was indicated to us by Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an influential Early Church Father known for his eloquent preaching.</note> in a passage recorded in the *Thesaurus* of the famous Suicer <note>Johann Caspar Suicer (1624–1684), a Swiss scholar who compiled a massive dictionary of Greek Christian terms.</note>: *“Everywhere the symbol of the cross stands before us; through...”* <note>original: "πανταχοῦ τὸ σύμβολον ἡμῖν τοῦ σταυροῦ παρίσταται· διὰ τοῦ" / "pantachou to symbolon hēmin tou staurou paristatai; dia tou"</note>

We inscribe the symbol of the cross everywhere with great zeal: on our houses, on the walls, on the windows, on our foreheads, and in our minds. <note>original Latin/Greek: "Ubique Symbolum crucis nobis adest. Ob eam cauſſam & in ædibus, & in parietibus, & in feneſtris, & in frontibus, & in animo ſtudioſè eam inſcribimus."</note>

<margin>
Section 64.
b. Commending oneself to the protection of Christ.
</margin>

Then, by that same sign, they commended themselves, as well as their modest belongings and all their actions, to the most excellent Savior, whose memory they were honoring. Chrysostom is cited again by the illustrious Suicer <note>Johann Caspar Suicer (1620–1684) was a Swiss theologian famous for his "Thesaurus Ecclesiasticus," a massive dictionary of the Greek Church Fathers.</note>: “When you are about to cross the threshold of the gateway, first say these words: ‘I renounce you, Satan, and your display <term>pompa<gloss>a public procession or showy display, often associated with pagan rituals or worldly vanity</gloss></term>, and your worship, and I join myself to you, Christ.’ Never leave home without saying this; it will be your staff, your weapon, and your unconquerable tower. Along with these words, form the sign of the cross upon your forehead.” <note>original Latin/Greek: "Januæ veſtibula transgreſſurus hoc prius dic verbum: Abrenuncio tibi Satana, & pompæ tuæ..."</note>

...and your worship, and I join myself to you, O Christ. <note>original Latin: "tuæ, & cultui tuo, & conjungor tibi Christe." <margin>
Section 65.
How they marked other limbs, utensils, houses, etc., with the cross.
</margin>

For this reason, they marked with the cross not only the forehead, but also other members of the body—especially those suffering from illness—as well as food, clothing, and household tools. <note>The early church viewed the cross as a "seal" that set apart everyday items for holy use and protection.</note> **Ephrem the Syrian**, page 537: *“Let us depict and engrave this life-giving sign on our doors, and on our foreheads, and eyes, and on our mouths and chests, and on all our limbs.”* <note>Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373 AD) was a prominent Syriac Christian deacon, hymnwriter, and theologian.</note> **Jerome**, writing of Paula in Epistle 8, book 3: *“When she marked her mouth and stomach, and strove to soothe her mother's pain with the impression of the cross, she was overcome by affection.”* <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD) was a priest and scholar best known for translating the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate). Paula was one of his most devoted followers and a pioneer of monastic life for women.</note> We recently heard of a bed marked with the cross from **Tertullian**, and of houses from **Chrysostom**. Furthermore, those in Alexandria who converted from the worship of **Serapis** to Christ painted the sign of the Lord’s cross on doorposts, entrances, windows, walls, and columns—

<note>Serapis was a Greco-Egyptian deity whose temple in Alexandria, the Serapeum, was destroyed by Christians in 391 AD. The discovery of cross-like symbols (the "ankh") in the temple was interpreted by early Christians as a prophecy of Christ.</note>

When that bread was already covered with coals and ashes, he said: 'Why did you not mark this?' Saying this, he made the sign of the cross with his finger toward the coals. As he made the sign, the bread immediately gave a massive crack, as if a huge jar had cracked in the fire.”*

<page-num>95</page-num>

...[the loaf of bread] had been. When it had been baked later and removed from the fire, it was found marked with that cross which was made not by physical touch, but by faith. <note>original: "quam non contactus sed fides fecit." This concludes the story from the previous page where a monk's prayer caused a cross to appear on bread that had not been manually marked before baking.</note>

<margin>Section 66. With the exception of pavements.</margin>

However, it was eventually considered a sacrilege <note>original: "nefas"</note> to mark floors with the sign of the cross. Regarding **Tiberius II**, who succeeded **Justin [II]** in the Roman Empire in the year 575, **Paul the Deacon** writes in book 17, chapter 2: <note>Tiberius II Constantine was a Byzantine Emperor known for his generosity. Paul the Deacon (c. 720–799) was a monk and historian who chronicled the history of the Lombards and the later Roman Empire.</note> *“One day, while walking through the palace, he saw a marble slab in the floor of the house on which the cross of the Lord was carved. He said: ‘We ought to fortify our foreheads and hearts with the cross of our Lord, and yet here we are, treading it under our feet.’ And faster than he had spoken, he ordered the slab to be removed.”*

More than a century later, the **Synod of Trullo** prohibited the same practice in Canon 73: <note>The Synod of Trullo (692 AD), also called the Quinisext Council, met in Constantinople to issue 102 canons regarding church discipline and life. It is highly regarded in the Eastern Orthodox tradition.</note> *“Since the life-giving cross has shown us salvation, we must take every care to render the honor due to that through which we have been saved from the ancient fall. Therefore, paying it veneration with mind, word, and sense, those depictions of the cross made by some on the floor...”* <note>original Greek: "τοῦ ζωοποιοῦ σταυροῦ δείξαντος ἡμῖν τὸ σωτήριον, πᾶσαν σπουδὴν ἡμᾶς τιθέναι χρή... τοὺς ἐν τῷ ἐδάφει τοῦ σταυροῦ τύπους ὑπό τινων κατασκευα-"</note>

<summary>This page discusses the transition from miraculous occurrences of the cross on food to the official prohibition of placing the cross on floors or pavements to prevent it from being stepped upon. It cites Emperor Tiberius II and the Synod of Trullo as historical authorities for this shift in practice.</summary>

...[Since the life-giving cross has shown us salvation, we ought to apply every care to give due honor to that through which we were saved from the ancient fall. We therefore decree that from now on, those who fashion the image of the cross upon the floor shall be excommunicated. <note>original: "ἀφορίζεσθαι" (aphorizesthai) — to be set apart or excommunicated from the church community.</note> Regarding these words, **Balsamon** <note>Theodore Balsamon (c. 1140–1195) was a famous commentator on Church Law and the Patriarch of Antioch; his commentaries are still used in Eastern Orthodox canon law.</note> says: *Some people used to engrave the sign of the cross in stone or some other material upon the floors of churches or other places.*

<margin>
§ 67.
c. Safety in dangers.
</margin>
Thirdly, by making the sign of the cross, they believed themselves safe from the snares of Satan and secure in dangers—not, to be sure, by the inherent power of the sign itself, but by the power of **Christ the Savior**, to whose care they commended themselves through that sign. And they conceived this hope in their minds because they had seen demons miraculously expelled from human bodies by the sign of the cross. **Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD) known for his prolific and vigorous defense of Christianity.</note> writes in *The Scorpion’s Sting* against the Gnostics: <note>The Gnostics were early religious groups that often claimed secret spiritual knowledge; Tertullian's *Scorpiace* (The Scorpion's Sting) was written to encourage Christians facing persecution.</note> *“Faith is our protection, provided it is not itself struck by a lack of confidence in immediately marking oneself [with the cross], and exorcising, and anointing the heel of the beast.”* <note>original: "bestiæ calcem." This is likely an allusion to Genesis 3:15, referring to the spiritual battle against the devil, symbolized by a serpent or beast.</note>

<margin>Why they marked [the forehead] with the cross?</margin>

This leads us to investigate the reason why the early Christians fortified their foreheads above all with the sign of the cross; whether experts in the history of the ancient Church have ever clearly explained this, I for my part do not know. For the reasons why they occasionally marked other limbs with the cross are obvious: namely, when those parts were in poor health, as we have heard from **Jerome** regarding **Paula** <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD) wrote a famous biography of St. Paula, a wealthy Roman widow who followed him to Bethlehem to lead a monastic life.</note>. However, they wished to specifically entrust the heart to Christ the Savior through this rite, so that evil thoughts might not emerge from there by night or day. Regarding this, **Prudentius** <note>Aurelius Prudentius Clemens (c. 348–413 AD) was a Roman Christian poet whose hymns remained popular for centuries.</note>—a writer already of the fifth century—sings sweetly in his hymn before sleep:

> See that, when sleep calls
> And you seek your chaste bed,
> The figure of the cross marks
> Your forehead and the place of your heart.
> The cross drives away every sin,
> The darkness flees from the cross;
> A mind consecrated by such a sign
> Knows not how to waver.

As far as the marking of the forehead is concerned, either I am mistaken, or the followers of Christ...

<header>H I S T O R Y</header>

...used it <note>Referring to the sign of the cross from the previous page.</note> in place of the custom by which the worshippers of false gods were accustomed to bind their heads with crowns, both to imitate and to honor them. For the ancient Church believed it a sacrilege <note>The Latin *nefas* implies an act against divine law or a profound moral outrage.</note> for Christians to encircle their temples with such wreaths, not only because that ceremony seemed to worship false deities, but also because, following the example of the Savior, a crown of thorns is more fitting for our brow, seeing as we have been called to bear the cross, and not to luxury, adornment, pomp, and joys. This is essentially the argument of the book which **Tertullian** <note>Tertullian (c. 155–220 AD) was a prolific early Christian author from Carthage known for his rigorous moral stance.</note> wrote, *On the Military Crown* <note>original: "De Corona militis"</note>. But in place of a crown of thorns which otherwise would have been placed on the head, they seem to have settled for the sign of the cross made upon the forehead. **Ephrem the Syrian** <note>Ephrem (c. 306–373 AD) was a prominent Syriac Christian deacon and hymnographer.</note> says in his *Sermon on the Lord's Cross*: *“The cross is the guardian of infants, the strength of men, and the crown of the elderly”* <note>original: "Crux infantium custos, virorum caput, senum corona"</note>. **Tertullian**, in the place cited above: *“If for these things you owe your head to Him* [<insert>Christ</insert>]*, repay Him with such a head as He offered His own for yours: either do not be crowned with flowers, if you cannot be crowned with thorns; for you cannot be crowned with flowers [while He was not]”* <note>original: "Si ob haec caput ei tuum debes, tale si forte ei repende, quale suum pro tuo obtulit: aut nec floribus coroneris, si spinis non potes: quia non floribus potes."</note>.

<margin>§. 69. Crosses suspended from the neck and attached to clothing:</margin>

Another use of the cross in the Early Church <note>original: "Veteri Ecclesia"</note>, although much more recent than the previous one <note>The "previous use" refers to the practice of marking the forehead with the sign of the cross mentioned on page 100.</note>, was that people wore crosses made of various materials attached to their clothes, or hanging from their necks, or even carried them in their hands. And indeed, the origin of this custom cannot be traced back beyond the times of **Constantine the Great** <note>The first Roman Emperor to convert to Christianity (reigned 306–337 AD).</note> and the Empress **Helena** <note>Saint Helena (c. 246–330 AD), mother of Constantine, who traditionally is credited with discovering the "True Cross" in Jerusalem.</note>. For after the Emperor's mother had either found the cross upon which the Jews had hung Jesus, or was believed to have found it, small pieces were cut from it to provide proof of the Savior's death. These were sent here and there, and by some they were kept quite superstitiously as venerable relics <note>term: <term>Relics</term> (Latin: *reliquiæ*), physical remains of a saint or objects they touched, believed to hold spiritual power.</note> and hung upon the body. Therefore others, who were not so fortunate as to visit Jerusalem <note>original: "Hierosolymas"</note> themselves, or to otherwise receive a portion of the life-giving wood <note>The "life-giving wood" (Latin: *salutari ligno*) is a traditional poetic way of referring to the wood of the True Cross.</note>, [would receive...]

<page-num>100</page-num>

<header>->HISTORY<-</header>

They used it [the sign of the cross] in place of the custom where worshippers of false gods were accustomed to bind their heads with crowns, both to imitate and to honor those deities. For the early Church believed it was a sin <note>original: "nefas"</note> for Christians to encircle their temples with garlands of this kind, not only because that ceremony seemed to worship false gods, but also because, following the Savior’s example, a crown of thorns better suits our brow, since we are called to bear the cross—not to luxury, finery, pomp, and joys. This is essentially the argument of the book which **Tertullian** <note>An influential early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD).</note> wrote *On the Soldier’s Crown* <note>original: "De Corona militis"</note>. Instead of a physical crown of thorns to be placed on the head at other times, they seem to have been satisfied with the sign of the cross made on the forehead. **Ephrem the Syrian** <note>A 4th-century Syriac deacon and prolific hymn-writer.</note> in his *Sermon on the Lord's Cross*: *“The Cross is the guardian of infants, the head of men, and the crown of the elderly.”* **Tertullian** in the place cited above: *“If for these reasons you owe your head to Him [Christ], repay Him with such a head, if perhaps you can, as He offered of His own for yours: either do not be crowned with flowers, if you cannot be crowned with thorns: because you cannot be crowned with flowers [at all].”*

<meta>catchword: you can [potes].</meta>

<header>->OF THE TEMPLARS.<-</header>

<sig>G 3</sig>

...you can <note>This continues the thought from the previous page regarding how a Christian should respond to the use of pagan-style crowns.</note> [say], *“Keep God's property unsullied for Him.”* <note>original: "Serva Deo rem suam intaminatam."</note> However, I will not stubbornly defend this conjecture, but will be very grateful to anyone who teaches more certain, or at least more probable, things.

<margin>Section 69. Crosses suspended from the neck and attached to clothing:</margin>

Another use of the cross in the Early Church <note>original: "Veteri Ecclesia"</note>, although much more recent than the previous one <note>The "previous use" refers to the practice of marking the forehead with the sign of the cross mentioned on page 100.</note>, was that people wore crosses made of various materials attached to their clothes, or hanging from their necks, or even carried them in their hands. And indeed, the origin of this custom cannot be traced back beyond the times of **Constantine the Great** <note>The first Roman Emperor to convert to Christianity (reigned 306–337 AD).</note> and the Empress **Helena** <note>Saint Helena (c. 246–330 AD), mother of Constantine, who traditionally is credited with discovering the "True Cross" in Jerusalem.</note>. For after the Emperor's mother had either found the cross upon which the Jews had hung Jesus, or was believed to have found it, small pieces were cut from it to provide proof of the Savior's death. These were sent here and there, and by some they were kept quite superstitiously as venerable <term>relics</term> <note>term: <term>Relics</term> (Latin: *reliquiæ*), physical remains of a saint or objects they touched, believed by many in the early and medieval church to hold spiritual power.</note>.

<summary>This text explores why early Christians rejected pagan floral crowns in favor of the sign of the cross and the "crown of thorns," and dates the origin of wearing physical cross jewelry to the era of Constantine and Helena's discovery of the True Cross.</summary>

Another use of the cross in the Early Church <note>original: "Vetus Ecclesia"</note>, although much more recent than the previously mentioned practice <note>The "previous practice" refers to the sign of the cross made with the hand on the forehead, discussed on the preceding page.</note>, was that people wore crosses made of various materials, either fastened to their clothing or hanging from their necks, or even carried them in their hands. For after the Emperor’s mother had either found the cross upon which the Jews had hung Jesus, or was believed to have found it, small fragments were cut from it to serve as evidence of the Savior's death. These were sent here and there, and were kept by some as venerable relics <term>reliquiae: physical remains of a saint or objects associated with Jesus</term>—perhaps somewhat superstitiously—and were hung upon the body. Having received this, and believing that the city where it was kept would be perfectly safe, he hid it within his own statue, which stands in the city of Constantine [Constantinople], in the so-called Forum of Constantine, atop the great porphyry <term>porphyry: a hard, purple-red stone used for imperial monuments</term> and massive column.”*

Concerning Urban II <note>Pope Urban II (reigned 1088–1099), who initiated the First Crusade at the Council of Clermont.</note> at the Council of Clermont, Guibert the Abbot <note>Guibert of Nogent (c. 1055–1124), a historian and theologian whose work "Gesta Dei per Francos" provides a primary account of the First Crusade.</note> writes in his *History of Jerusalem*, Book 1, Chapter 5: *“He instituted a sufficiently fitting sign for this most honorable profession, acting as a belt of knighthood: or rather, for those about to serve as soldiers for God, he bestowed the mark <note>original: "stigma," referring here to a brand or symbolic wound</note> of the Lord’s passion. He commanded that the figure of a cross, made from cloth of any material, be sewn onto the tunics, hooded cloaks, and mantles of those who were to go.”*

Baldric <note>Baldric of Dol (c. 1050–1130), an archbishop and historian of the First Crusade.</note>, in Book 1 of his *History of Jerusalem*, writes: *“Immediately, everyone sewed the banner of the holy cross onto their outer garments. For so the Pope had commanded; and it had pleased those about to depart to make this sign: indeed, the Supreme Pontiff had preached that the Lord said to his followers: 'If anyone does not carry his cross and follow me, he cannot be my disciple.'”* <note>A reference to the Gospel of Luke 14:27.</note>

Fulcher of Chartres <note>Fulcher of Chartres (c. 1059–1127), a priest who participated in the First Crusade and served as chaplain to Baldwin I of Jerusalem.</note>, Book 1, Chapter 1: *“O how worthy and pleasant it was for all of us to behold those crosses, whether made of silk or...”*

<margin>§. 72. Crosses carried in the hands.</margin>
A later age finally began the practice of carrying the cross in the hands, and indeed for various reasons. Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), the Archbishop of Constantinople known for his eloquence and his role in shaping Christian liturgy.</note> was the first to command that a silver cross be carried before those who sang that the glory of the Son was equal to the Majesty of the Father during nightly supplications. Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus (c. 380–439), a Greek Christian historian who continued the work of Eusebius.</note>, Book 6, Chapter 8: *“For silver crosses had been devised by him, bearing...”* <note>original Greek: "Ἐπινενόηντο γὰρ παρ’ αὐτοῦ σταυροὶ ἀργυροῖ φέροντες..." This refers to the introduction of processional crosses to counter the public hymns and processions of the Arians in Constantinople.</note>

Patriarchs, Archbishops, and Bishops have the same right, though not in the presence of a Papal Legate <note>A personal representative of the Pope.</note> or a Cardinal. 

In the year 1279, a discord was stirred up between the Archbishops of Canterbury and York, because the Archbishop of York had a cross carried before him in the province of Canterbury; and it was forbidden by the Archbishop of Canterbury for anyone to sell food to the Archbishop of York or to enter into any contracts with him <note>This refers to the long-standing dispute over primacy between the two major English sees; carrying the cross was a sign of jurisdictional authority.</note>. *On the day before the Calends of May <note>April 30th.</note>, after Wales had been subjugated, King Edward <note>Edward I (r. 1272–1307), who conquered Wales in 1282–1283.</note>, coming to Westminster, was preceded by the Archbishop of Canterbury and his <term>suffragan bishops</term> <note>Bishops who are subordinate to an Archbishop.</note> dressed in their vestments. This likely refers to the "Croes Naid," a relic of the True Cross held by the Welsh princes which Edward seized as a symbol of his victory.</note>, decorated with gold, silver, and precious stones, which he had brought with him from Wales, to Westminster with a solemn procession and gathering. The same author [states that] Innocent III <note>Pope from 1198–1216.</note>, after the Albigensians <note>A group in Southern France deemed heretical by the Catholic Church; they were the target of the Albigensian Crusade.</note> had been defeated by soldiers signed with the cross <note>Crusaders.</note>, established the Order of the <term>Cruciferi</term> <note>Literally "Cross-bearers."</note>, who were called monks because they carried crosses upon staffs. Matthew Paris <note>A celebrated 13th-century English chronicler and monk of St. Albans.</note> records that they first came to England in the year 1244. However, I cannot say when parish priests first began to carry a cross before funeral processions, or to carry it around during the <term>lustrations</term> <note>Ritual purifications or blessings, often referring to "Rogation Days" where a priest walked the boundaries of the parish fields.</note> of the fields.*

<header>HISTORY</header>

<margin>marked with the sign by Constantine.</margin>...[the] deed of Constantine the Great <note>Reigned 306–337 AD; the first Roman emperor to convert to Christianity.</note>, who marked the <term>labara</term> <note>A military standard (flag) used in the Roman Empire, which Constantine adapted to include Christian symbols.</note> with the sign of the cross, having been warned in a divine dream, as historians report. Lactantius <note>An early Christian author (c. 250 – c. 325 AD) who was an advisor to Constantine.</note>, in *On the Deaths of the Persecutors*, chapter 44 [writes]: *Constantine was warned in his sleep to mark the heavenly sign of God on the shields, and so join the battle. He did as he was commanded, and with the letter X turned sideways, with the top of its head bent around <note>This describes the "Chi-Rho" monogram (☧), where the Greek letter Rho (P) is superimposed on the Chi (X), representing the first two letters of "Christ" in Greek.</note>, he marked "Christ" on the shields. Armed with this sign, the army took up the sword.* <note>original: "transversa X litera, summo capite circumflexo, Christum in scutis notat. Quo signo armatus exercitus capit ferrum."</note> See also Theodoret <note>A 5th-century bishop and influential theologian/historian.</note>, book 1, chapter 8. Gothofredus <note>Jacques Godefroy (1587–1652), a prominent jurist and editor of historical texts.</note>, in his notes on Philostorgius <note>An Arian church historian of the 4th and 5th centuries.</note>, book 1, chapter 6, leans toward the opinion of those who count this narrative among fables; but his minor observations are not of such weight that they draw me into agreement. For if facts are to be called into doubt whenever authors vary in recounting their circumstances, we shall have little or nothing certain regarding ancient matters: and what accurate investigator of events does not observe that those things which happen most truly in our own age are told differently by different people?

<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>OF THE TEMPLARS.</header>

...[to be consid]ered? Still, I do not believe that Constantine’s vision should be regarded as a great miracle. After all, why should we be so surprised that a military commander would dream of a banner, the emblem upon that banner, and his coming victory? In fact, ancient authors rightly observed that the Romans had already fashioned their banners in a shape that closely resembled a cross. 

Justin Martyr <note>An early Christian philosopher and apologist (c. 100–165 AD) who wrote defenses of Christianity addressed to the Roman Emperors.</note> in his *Second Apology*: *Indeed, even your standards—I speak of the emblems on your banners and other insignias—and your trophies <note>A "tropaeum" was a monument erected to celebrate a military victory, typically consisting of captured arms and armor hung on a wooden frame with a cross-beam.</note>, through which your public processions are conducted everywhere, display and demonstrate the power of this very shape [the cross] as signs of your empire and authority, even if you do so without realizing it.* <note>original: "Quin & signa vestra figuræ hujus vim præferunt & declarant (vexillorum signa dico aliorumque insignium) & tropæorum, per quæ progressus à vobis in publicum quovis locorum fiunt, imperii & potestatis indicia in hisce ostendentibus, licet hoc non intelligentes faciatis."</note> 

Minutius Felix <note>A Roman lawyer and Christian apologist, likely writing in the 2nd or 3rd century AD.</note> in his work *Octavius*: *As for crosses, we neither worship them nor pray to them. It is you, surely—you who consecrate wooden gods—who perhaps adore wooden crosses as if they were parts of your gods. For your very standards, and your <term>cantabra</term> <note>A richly decorated Roman military standard, often featuring a square piece of cloth hanging from a horizontal bar.</note>, and the banners of your camps—what are they but gilded and decorated crosses? Your victorious trophies do not*

<header>HISTORY</header>

*...not only the appearance of a simple cross, but they also imitate that of a man affixed to one:* <note>original: "tantum simplicis crucis faciem, verùm & affixi hominis imitantur"</note> Compare Tertullian <note>An influential early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD) known for his "Apology" defending the faith against Roman accusations.</note> in his *Apology*, chapter 16. Furthermore, that Constantine interpreted this sign on the banners as the cross so celebrated among Christians could have happened because he knew that the Christian people placed all their trust in the Savior who was once fixed to a cross, and hoped for help from him alone. He may have seen that this Savior was not actually an evil-doer, but was innocent and a devoted worshiper of his God—one who, though indeed oppressed by long-standing misery, was nevertheless miraculously preserved and his followers increased. Therefore, Constantine determined in his mind to make a trial of that same trust and hope. When this succeeded happily and his enemies were defeated, he publicly gave his name to Christ <note>Meaning he officially converted to Christianity.</note>. 

Whether a bright trophy of the cross actually appeared to him in the sky around midday, I leave that an open question. <note>original: "id in medio relinquo" — literally "I leave it in the middle," a Latin idiom meaning the author is remaining neutral or undecided on the truth of the miracle.</note> At the very least, writers of a later age mention that something similar happened on various occasions. Paul the Deacon <note>An 8th-century Benedictine monk and historian who wrote a famous history of the Lombards and a "Miscellaneous History" of Rome.</note> in his *Miscellaneous History*, book 11, chapter 20, records such an event involving Gallus <note>Constantius Gallus, a member of the Constantinian dynasty who served as Caesar (junior emperor) from 351–354 AD.</note> at Antio- <note>The text cuts off here at "Antio-", likely referring to the city of Antioch.</note>

*...coming to Antioch [in the year 351], a sign appeared in the sky around the East on the day of Pentecost. Matthew Paris* <note>A famous 13th-century English historian and monk of St. Albans.</note> *writes for the year 1217 that the province of Cologne was brought to Christ by signs of this kind.*

<margin>§. 74. Crosses established in public ways and temples.</margin>
Finally, the third use of the cross in the early Church—by which they fixed larger wooden crosses in public ways, in temples, before temples, and elsewhere—drew its first origin from the same Constantine.

<page-num>114</page-num>
# HISTORY #

Indeed, Eusebius explicitly states in his *On the Praises of Constantine*: "For the Emperor honored the victory-giving sign, having learned through action the experience of the divinity within it... and finally, as if paying a debt for the cumulative fulfillment of all the good things we enjoy, the Emperor established triumphal monuments everywhere on earth." <note>original Greek: "ὁ δὲ βασιλεὺς τὸ νικοποιὸν ἐτίμα σημεῖον..." and the Latin translation provided in the text: "Imperator autem triumfale signum honorat..."</note> Shortly thereafter, it became custom that those places where people wished to build temples, oratories, or monasteries were dedicated by fixing a cross in the ground. Regarding this practice, there exist the decrees of the Legal Scholar Julian <note>referring to Julianus Antecessor, a 6th-century professor of law at Constantinople</note> and the *Novels* <note>New legal constitutions</note> of the Emperor Justinian, which are cited by Charles du Fresne <note>often called Du Cange, a renowned 17th-century scholar of medieval history</note> and the celebrated Suicer <note>Johann Caspar Suicer, a scholar of Greek ecclesiastical terminology</note>. It was considered a sacrilege to begin the construction of a sacred building unless the <term>stauropegeion<gloss>from the Greek 'stauros' (cross) and 'pegnymi' (to fix or plant); the ritual of planting a cross to mark the site of a future church</gloss></term> had first taken place. As stated in the *Capitularies* <note>a collection of royal ordinances</note> of Charlemagne, chapter 229: "**No one shall build a church before the Bishop of the city comes and publicly fixes a cross in that very place.**" Crosses were also placed in public ways,

<margin>§. 76.
The Emblem.</margin>
Regarding their emblem, Matthew Paris <note>a celebrated 13th-century English chronicler and monk</note> writes in his *Chronicle*: "**At first they were so poor, though valiant, that those two [Hugh de Payens and Godfrey de Saint-Omer] had only a single warhorse between them; therefore, in memory of their original poverty and to encourage the practice of humility, two men riding one horse were engraved upon their seal.**" <note>original Latin: "Primò adeo pauperes, licet strenui, fuerunt..."</note> A ***dextrarius*** <term>destrier: the most famous type of medieval warhorse, bred for strength and stamina in battle</term>, more accurately called a *destrarius* from the Celtic word *destrier*, is a larger and armored horse, used primarily in wars and battles; others say it is named from the "right hand" (*dextra*), because it was led by the right hand until the time for combat arrived.

Strict Discipline.</margin>
From the beginning, they observed the rules of their order diligently and rigidly. ...from the beginning, guarding themselves and looking to the future, they were in no way willing to ignore or let go unpunished the transgressions or negligence of brothers who went astray. Instead, they diligently weighed the gravity of the crimes and the circumstances of the sins with a careful examination. They would irrevocably cast some out from their fellowship and strip them of the **red cross** <note>The red cross was the Order's most sacred symbol; losing it meant a permanent loss of status and identity as a Templar</note>, lest the flock of sheep be stained by the contagion of a diseased goat. Others, however, they ordered to eat meager food on the ground without a tablecloth until they had made worthy satisfaction <note>original: "condignam satisfactionem," referring to penance that is equal to the offense committed</note>, so that shame would be instilled in them before everyone, and fear in the others. To these men—to increase their embarrassment and for their atonement—it was not permitted even to drive away the dogs if they happened to eat with them while they were on the floor. Others still, so that they might be freed from the **prison of hell** <term>gehennali carcere: a reference to Gehenna, the biblical place of punishment, used here to mean that earthly prison saves the soul from eternal damnation</term>, they restrained with prisons and chains for a time, or forever, according to what seemed necessary. In many other ways besides, according to the [discipline] of the salutary rule...

<margin>§. 78. Obedience.</margin>

*They also humbly showed due obedience and reverence to the Lord **Patriarch of Jerusalem** <note>The highest-ranking Christian official in the Holy Land, who provided the Templars with spiritual authority and physical support</note>, from whom they received both the beginning of their religious profession and the support for their physical lives: rendering tithes and other things that are God’s to God, and those things that are Caesar’s to Caesar <note>original: "quæ sunt Dei, Deo, & quæ sunt Cæsaris, Cæsari reddentes" — a reference to the famous biblical command regarding taxes and religious duty</note>. They were a nuisance to no one, but were loved by all for their humility and devotion. (The same [author, James of Vitry]). And Bernard, in his **Exhortation to the Knights**, chapter 4 <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux was the Order's most influential early patron and helped draft their Rule</note>: First, in either situation [at war or at home], discipline is never lacking, and obedience is by no means disregarded; for according to the testimony of Scripture, "a son without discipline shall perish" <note>original: "filius indisciplinatus peribit," likely referring to Ecclesiasticus 22:3</note>, and "it is a sin of witchcraft to rebel, and like the crime of idolatry to refuse to submit" <note>original: "peccatum est ariolandi repugnare..." — a reference to 1 Samuel 15:23</note>. They come and go at the command of the one in charge; they wear what he has provided, and no clothing or food is sought from any other source.*

<margin>§. 79.
*Diligence.*</margin>
When they were free from war, they labored with their hands. **Bernard**, in the same place cited <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux, *In Praise of the New Knighthood* (Exhortatio ad milites Templi)</note>: *At no time do they sit idly or wander about out of curiosity; but always, when they are not on the move—which indeed happens rarely—they mend torn parts of their armor or clothing, repair what is old, or put in order what is messy, so that they do not eat their bread for free <note>original: "ne gratis comedant panem" — a reference to earning one's keep through labor rather than charity alone</note>; and finally, they do whatever the will of the Master and common necessity dictates.*

<margin>§. 80.
*Integrity of Character.*</margin>
The same **Bernard** praises their integrity of character, in which they initially excelled beyond the typical lot of soldiers. *An insolent word,* he says, *a useless task, immoderate laughter, or even a faint murmur or whispering is by no means left uncorrected there. They detest chess and dice <note>original: "Scacos & aleas" — games of chance and strategy were often banned for monks and knights to avoid the sins of anger and gambling</note>, they abhor hunting, and they find no delight in the typical playful sport of hawking <note>original: "ludicra illa avium rapina" — literally "the playful plunder of birds," referring to the aristocratic sport of falconry</note>. They reject and abominate mimes, magicians, and storytellers, scurrilous songs, and public spectacles, regarding them as vanities and false follies.*

<margin>§. 81. Prudence and strength in war.</margin>
In war they conducted themselves most commendably, at the nod and command of the one who oversees the military service, not impulsively or in a disorderly fashion, but proceeding to battle prudently and with all caution. They were the first in advancing and the last in retreating; nor was it permitted for them to turn their backs in flight or to return without a command. <note>Attributed to Jacques de Vitry (c. 1160–1240), a chronicler and Bishop of Acre who wrote extensively on the Crusades.</note>

<margin>§. 82. Good reputation in the world.</margin>
Because of this, it happened that the name of their integrity and the fame of their holiness—like a spice cabinet redolent with a sweet fragrance—was spread throughout the entire world; the house of the Holy Church was filled with the scent of their ointment <note>original: "odore unguenti" — a biblical metaphor for virtue and divine favor</note>; and the memory of them in the mouths of all the faithful tasted like the sweetness of honey. *The same.* <note>Referring again to Jacques de Vitry.</note>

<margin>§ 85. The wealth of the Templars:</margin>

<margin>Section 87. The causes of generosity toward Clergy, Monks, and the Holy Knights.</margin>
It is of no help to gather more edicts of Emperors, Kings, and Republics on this matter, whether ancient or more recent; but it is worth the effort to inquire into the causes which brought it about that the Church—and especially the monks, clergy, and <term>religious knights</term> <note>original: "Militesque religiosi"; members of military orders like the Templars who took religious vows of poverty, chastity, and obedience</note>—became so enriched. And as I think diligently upon these things, it becomes clear to me that the men of the clergy and of other Orders omitted nothing that might serve to acquire wealth; and furthermore, that the minds of those who so lavishly distributed or poured out their wealth were so disposed that they could very easily be stirred to exercise such <term>generosity</term> <note>original: "liberalitatem"; in a legal and social sense, the voluntary gifting of assets</note>.

Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 342–420 AD) was a priest and theologian best known for translating the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate); he was often a harsh critic of clerical corruption.</note>, in his *Epistle*, described these sordid matters aptly, though they are indeed sordid. ***They kiss*** [he is speaking of the Roman Clergy] ***the heads of the matrons*** <note>original: "matronarum"; wealthy, high-status married women in Roman society</note>, ***and with hand extended—so that you might think they wish to bless them, if you did not know better—they receive payment for their greetings — — — certain ones have devoted their entire zeal and life to this: knowing the names, homes, and habits of the matrons. Of these, I shall briefly describe one who is the master of this craft — — — He rises hurriedly with the sun; the order of his visiting is arranged; shortcuts through the streets are sought out, and the troublesome old man enters almost into the very bedrooms of those still sleeping. If he sees a little cushion, an elegant tablecloth*** <note>original: "mantile"; can refer to a napkin, towel, or decorative cloth</note>, ***or any piece of household furniture, he praises it, admires it, handles it, and, complaining that he lacks such things, he does not so much request them as extort them.*** Again, concerning Priests and Monks: ***I hear of the shameful servitude of certain men toward old men and women who lack children. # HISTORY OF THE TEMPLARS

<page-num>135</page-num>

<header>OF THE TEMPLARS</header>

...church <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page: "those who deserved well of the church."</note> who had earned it. For they used to honor the living with the most attentive services, pointing them out with a finger as living examples of perfected piety. They praised the dead most eloquently in public sermons and placed them among the Saints of the first rank; they inscribed their names in the calendars <note>original: "fastis"; the official lists of saints and feast days</note> and committed their bodies to the tomb with veneration, pomp, groans, tears, and lamentations. They refreshed their souls in the fires of purgatory <note>The Catholic doctrine of a place of purification after death; the author suggests the clergy used the promise of shortening this time to encourage donations.</note> with sacrifices, prayers, and supplications, or even summoned them forth from it.

<margin>Section 95.
β. On the part of the givers.</margin>
From all these things, I believe it is clearly established that the leaders of the Church omitted nothing of those things which serve to obtain wealth. Now, the second point must also be shown, which I proposed at the beginning: namely, that the minds of Christians were at that time so disposed that they could easily be moved to bestow goods upon the Church.

<margin>Section 96.
Joy regarding the received peace.</margin>

# HISTORY

<page-num>136</page-num>

<header>HISTORY</header>

...having recently acquired a good <note>The text continues from the previous page: "He who has..."</note>, one most gladly spends what must necessarily be spent for the enjoyment <note>original: "fruitionem"</note> of this good. We see this clearly when citizens devastated by war are most ready to dedicate the remnants of their shattered fortunes to restoring the ruins of cities, towns, villages, and temples as soon as some hope of peace and freedom from the enemy appears.

<margin>Section 97. Remembrance of past times.</margin>
Furthermore, the careful remembrance <note>original: "recordatio"</note> of past times—during which all followers of Christ in general, and especially the clergy <note>original: "Viri Ecclesiastici"</note>, had lived in misery—could not fail to stir a generous compassion in wealthy men. Moved by this, they sought to do good for those who had endured so many and such great evils for the name of Jesus for so long, and who were still leading a life of poverty.

<margin>Section 98. The noble opinion concerning the sanctity of generosity.</margin>
A third factor was the very noble opinion with which their minds were imbued regarding wealth poured out generously into the lap of the Church. Specifically, it was held to be the very height of piety <note>original: "apex pietatis"</note>...

# OF THE TEMPLARS.

<page-num>137</page-num>

<header>OF THE TEMPLARS</header>

<sig>I 5</sig>

charity was held <note>original: "habebatur charitas"; this completes the sentence from the previous page: "it was held to be the very height of piety..."</note>; and who was more worthy of charity <note>original: "charitas"; here meaning Christian love and the giving that flows from it</note> than our holy mother, the Church, and her firstborn sons, the Clergy and the Monks? *Good works* <note>original: "opera bona"</note> were requested, demanded, and urged: and lest anyone be deceived, this august and sweet name of "good works" was considered to apply only to generous gifts, made either during one’s life or after death. Who, I ask, is so sluggish and lazy that he would deny that one must work? Who is so wicked as to argue that one should not work well? But reach your hand into your bag, pay out the coins, and make a will <note>term: "testamentum"; a legal document for the distribution of property after death</note> that is useful to those petitioning you: now you are "working well." You work better if you give more, and you work best of all if, whether living or dead, you give everything. This was the doctrine, the opinion, and the religion of those times.

<margin>Section 99. And on its utility.</margin>
And what high hopes had they not conceived regarding the fruits of this generosity? While we live, it is a fine thing to be pointed out with a finger and for it to be said, "There he is!" <note>The author uses the phrase "hic est!", a classical allusion to the desire for public recognition and fame.</note> "This is the man who establishes religion, who preserves the Church, and who nourishes the Church’s ministers! Here is he who founds temples and institutes Monasteries!"

# HISTORY

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>HISTORY</header>
<meta>catchword: aucto- (the beginning of "auctoritate" or "authority")</meta>

...protects and enriches <note>original: "tuetur, ditat"; completing the thought from the previous page that a donor "protects and enriches religion and the Church."</note>. Yet it is more glorious to be read in the public records <note>original: "fastis"; referring to the calendars of festivals or official lists of names</note> after death, to be praised before the assembly, and to be celebrated in the annals. It is more blessed to be remembered among the Saints, even among the Holiest: to deserve eternal life, and to wear the victor’s crown <term>laureolam; in medieval theology, a "little laurel" or crown of secondary glory given to specific groups like martyrs, virgins, or teachers in addition to the primary reward of heaven</term>. 

But oh, wretched soul! How will you deserve that life, and where will you wear that crown? You are tormented in purgatory, or you will be tortured among those in the underworld <note>original: "inferos"; the inhabitants of the lower regions or Hell</note>. Give your coins, enrich the priests, sacrifice your wealth <note>original: "macta reditibus"; literally to slaughter or offer up your incomes/rents as a sacrifice</note> to the Monks, and you shall escape Gehenna <note>original: "gehennam"; a biblical term for the place of fiery punishment</note>. You will not remain in purgatory; you will be summoned from there, led out, and drawn forth by a silver chain and a golden rope. While you remain there, you will be aided by sacrifices <note>original: "sacrificiis"; specifically the celebration of the Mass offered for the souls of the dead</note>, refreshed by prayers, and gladdened by song. That place is dark, dismal, and unpleasant, O soul, yet to that place you must go. Give, therefore, what you have; give quickly, give willingly; if you have much, give all. If you have nothing, see to it that you get something; buy your freedom, or at least buy mercy for your captivity! This was the hope of those times, and this was the despair!

They claim that they follow and hold those very principles which, by a saving power, drove the ancient Christians to produce so many monuments of holy generosity <note>original: "liberalitatis"; the virtue of giving freely</note>—making donations and wills that were useful to the sacred Orders and ecclesiastical figures, and necessary for building or repairing churches. They claim that they are the worthy sons succeeding those Fathers who were so generous toward God. They argue that the desire to possess, acquire, increase, and keep earthly riches is naturally implanted in human minds; therefore, they claim a doctrine must undoubtedly be divine if it is strong enough to shake the love of wealth out of people and make works of charity—which are otherwise most difficult and contrary to our natural desires—so easy to perform.

<header>OF THE CHURCH-BUILDERS</header>

...on as if they were dealing with stupid or dull-witted people <note>original: "bardisque"; referring to people who are slow to understand or foolish</note> who neither know the facts, nor understand the nature of things, nor are able to judge them based on their actual causes. As if our great, holy, and just God would sell the worship due to Him for money! As if He would trade the purity of life He requires of us for a distribution of resources <note>original: "facultatum"; assets or property</note>! As if the donation of goods that are about to be left behind anyway [could be] thought <note>the word "cogi-" continues on the next page, likely as "cogitari," meaning to be thought of or considered</note> as holiness—

<margin>§ 103. The sources of the generosity of the Ancients:</margin> We easily dismiss the unfairness of those who falsely claim <note>original: "calumnientur"; to bring false charges or slandering</note> that, by arguing this way, the praise of pious generosity is being diminished, and that works of charity are either being discouraged entirely or are not being honored as they deserve. Yet I believe my arguments are all the more correct because I see so clearly that the generosity of the ancients, alas, too often flowed from no other source than superstition, ignorance, and a lack of true faith <note>original: "impietate"</note>. For people who were otherwise stingy and infrequent worshipers of God believed—whether out of a lack of faith or sheer ignorance—that they had properly fulfilled all the duties of a good Christian and had provided well for their soul’s salvation when, at the end of their lives, they bequeathed their wealth <note>original: "substantias"</note> (which was not infrequently acquired through violence and fraud) to "pious causes" <note>original: "pias caussas"; legal or ecclesiastical foundations for charity or religious work</note>.

### OF THE TEMPLARS

<page-num>143</page-num>

<margin>§ 104. Especially regarding the Templars.</margin> I will point out in just a few words how it came to pass that, among the other orders, our Templars (for my discourse now returns to them) acquired such immense wealth in so short a time. And here, indeed, it is appropriate to recall what we mentioned at the start: that sacred pilgrimages, which were made violently unsafe by the incursions of the Turks and Arabs, gave birth to this religious knighthood <note>original: "religiosæ isti militiæ"; referring to the military orders like the Templars who took monastic vows</note>. For people, deceived by superstitious errors, burned with an irrational <note>original: "insano"; literally 'insane' or 'unhealthy', used here to describe the author’s view of excessive relic-veneration</note> desire to visit Palestine; they considered having been to Jerusalem and having visited <note>original: "salutasse"; literally 'to have greeted'</note> the tomb of the Lord as one of the primary requirements for holiness. 

Yet the Heavenly Father had subjected that land—once stained with the blood of His Son—to a fierce and barbaric people <note>A reference to the expansion of Islamic empires into the Levant</note>, after the ancient inhabitants <note>A reference to the Jewish people, whom the author describes as being dispersed as a consequence of the Crucifixion</note>, who were guilty of so great a crime, had been cast out and scattered far and wide. This new people made incredible progress in a short space of time, carrying their victorious arms everywhere, not only in Asia and Africa but also in Europe, inasmuch as...

<page-num>144</page-num>

...because they had been raised and instructed by God to punish the impiety of the Christians. For this reason, they were viewed with the same eyes through which we are usually accustomed to regard a people prepared to carry out the judgments of God. No one thought of amending their life, of casting aside errors, of purifying worship, or of restoring religion; instead, everyone accused and cursed the enemy. Kings and Princes gathered together, conscripted soldiers, struck treaties, scraped together funds <note>original: "numos corradere"; a vivid Latin phrase for the desperate or aggressive collection of money to fund the Crusades</note>, and set out for Palestine. Monks and all those who spoke to congregations from the pulpits <note>original: "ex alto"; literally 'from on high'</note> cried out: that the Turks must be destroyed, the haters of the Christian name extinguished, and the unjust masters of the Holy Land removed. This was, in effect, trying to cut off the very hand of God with which He wished to discipline them. I will not speak of the outcome of these equally foolish and wicked plans; but since the minds of Christians, especially the Latins <note>referring to Western Europeans who followed the Roman/Latin Rite, as opposed to Eastern/Greek Christians</note>, were so inclined in that unhappy age, who will wonder that they showered astounding wealth upon the soldiers who, for the sake of destroying the Saracens <note>a medieval term for Muslims</note>, in place—

*...acting quite prudently according to their own profession <note>The "profession" refers to their monastic vows</note>, they neglected humility—which is known to be the guardian of all virtues and which, by sitting willingly in the lowest place, has no ground from which to suffer a fall—and withdrew themselves from the Lord Patriarch of Jerusalem, from whom they had received both the founding of their order and their first benefits, denying him the obedience that their predecessors had shown to him.* <note>original: "profeſſioni ſuæ ſatis prudenter facientes... neglecta humilitate... domino Patriarchæ Hierofolymitano... ſe ſubtraxerunt"</note> Matthew Paris in his *Chronicle*. <note>Matthew Paris (c. 1200–1259) was a Benedictine monk and a famous English chronicler known for his detailed histories</note> While the order was flourishing, therefore, and prospering in humility, the enemy of human prosperity <note>A common medieval reference to the Devil</note> grew envious and sowed seeds that are seen to be contrary to this virtue: namely, the offshoots of pride, which usually produce disobedience along with schisms and many other evils. — — — *As an abundance of things produced insolence, they now forgot their profession and, neglecting humility, withdrew from the obedience of the Lord Patriarch, from whom they had received the Order’s institution, residence, protection, and all their first benefits; interven-* <note>original: "intervenien-"; the word is cut off and continues on the next page as "interveniente," likely referring to "intervening pride" or "intervening circumstances"</note>

§. 109.
Monks withdrawn from the jurisdiction of Bishops.
</margin>

On this occasion, we cannot help but recall the ambition of the Roman Pontiffs, by which they were incited to invade the jurisdiction of others; they always took it to heart to exempt the holy Orders from the obedience due to their superiors. In this way, the monks were originally subjected to the bishops by the decrees of many Councils. For having been employed by the Bishops to manage various affairs, they turned into "meddlers" <note>original Greek: πολυπράγμονες (*polypragmones*), a term for those who interfere in business outside their station or cell.</note> and came to despise the authority of those very Bishops. The First Council of Orléans <note>A church council held in 511 AD in Gaul (modern France) to establish ecclesiastical laws under the Merovingian kings.</note>, Canon 8, states: *Abbots shall be subject to Bishops, and Monks to Abbots.* <note>original Latin: "Abbates Episcopis, Monachi Abbatibus sint subjecti."</note> And the Second Council of Orléans <note>Held in 533 AD.</note>, Canon 21: *Abbots who despise the commands of Bishops shall not be admitted to communion, unless they set aside their stubbornness by embracing humility.* <note>original Latin: "Abbates qui Episcoporum præcepta despiciunt, ad communionem ne admittantur, nisi contumaciam humilitate suscepta deponant."</note> But later, having gained the power to elect their own Abbots, they gradually withdrew themselves from the power of the Bishops—by whom they did not wish to be kept in order—and especially in the time of Bernard <note>Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian leader who exerted massive influence over church politics.</note>, they purchased their freedom from the Roman Popes with money, promising in return the most devoted obedience to them.

It was said they kept others away with the intention that, by being the first to enter, they might seize the greatest spoils and the richest booty.* <note>This refers to a famous and controversial incident during the Siege of Ascalon in 1153, where the Templars were accused of blocking other Crusaders from entering a breach in the walls so they could loot the city alone.</note> Therefore, the besieged defenders, seeing the small number of those who had broken into the city, regained their courage and slaughtered the Templars they had cut off, according to William of Tyre, Book 17, chapter 27. Their Master, however, was taken prisoner by Saladin <note>The author here likely conflates different events or Masters; Bernard de Tremelay is generally believed to have died in the breach at Ascalon in 1153. A later Master, Bertrand de Blanchefort, was captured by Nur ad-Din—Saladin’s predecessor—in 1157.</note>, and was finally restored to liberty in the year 1157 through the mediation of Manuel, the Eastern Emperor <note>Manuel I Komnenos, the Byzantine Emperor.</note>. In the very next year, 1158, the Saracens found Baldwin III unprepared, as he had dismissed his victorious army too quickly...

<margin>§. 114. A cruel type of death:</margin>
In the year 1180, Saladin, after taking the castle at Jacob's Ford <note>A strategic crossing of the Jordan River where the Templars had built a fortress called Chastellet.</note> by force, cut the knights of the Temple in half with iron blades and condemned the remaining priests to death: *Génébrard in his Chronicle.* <note>Gilbert Génébrard was a 16th-century scholar; this reflects later historical compilations of these events.</note> After a conflict began between the Christians and Saladin, many died on both sides, yet more among the Christians, and the Master of the Temple was captured. When Saladin wished to exchange him for his own nephew, whom the Christians were holding captive, the Master of the Temple—this was Odo of Saint Amand—refused, saying *it is not the custom of the knights of the Temple that any ransom be given for them except for a belt and a knife* <note>original: "non esse consuetudinem militum templi, ut aliqua redemtio daretur pro eis præter cingulum & cultellum." This famous quote highlights the Templars' strict code of martyrdom and refusal to use Order funds for ransoms.</note>, and so he died in captivity. *Chronicle of Sigebert.*

<margin>§ 117. They are defeated in battle.</margin>
In the year 1188, on the 4th of October, a battle was joined between Saladin and Guy, the last King of Jerusalem. In this engagement, the Knights of the Temple—already devoted to the slaughter—had broken through the enemy ranks. When the Templars had pushed further forward, following their courage and their luck, the townsfolk of Acre <note>original: "Acconitæ"; residents of the strategic port city of Akko.</note> suddenly rushed upon them. Although the many overwhelmed the few, they triumphed only after a great slaughter of their own men. There, the Master of the Order, Gerard de Ridefort <note>original: "Gerhardus de Bidesfortia"; the 10th Grand Master of the Templars, known for his aggressive stance against Saladin.</note>, fell dead. See the writings of James of Vitry <note>original: "Jacob. de Vitr."; a 13th-century chronicler and Bishop of Acre who wrote the 'Historia Hierosolymitana'.</note>. We note in passing that Guy had promised Saladin—by whom he was held in chains at Damascus for nearly a full year—under oath that, once released from prison, he would surrender the kingdom and go into exile across the sea. However, Guy was persuaded by the Clergy—who judged that agreements made under the threat of fear were invalid <note>The Church often used the legal principle that oaths made under duress were not binding.</note>—and he broke his word to the Sultan of Egypt.
