# HISTORY

Matthew Paris, in his account of the reign of Henry III, for the year 1244.

<margin>§. 120. They are forbidden from making peace with the Sultan.</margin>

In the year 1248. *When the Master of the Templars had written from Syria to King Louis stating that the Egyptian Sultan had sent one of his satraps* <note>original: "satrapis"; here referring to a high-ranking official or governor</note> *to him to negotiate for peace, if the French would allow it: King Lusignan* <note>Hugh XI of Lusignan, Count of La Marche, a French nobleman who accompanied Saint Louis on the Seventh Crusade.</note>*, who was experienced in the affairs, the people, and those regions and could not be deceived, said—when the matter was being discussed in council—that he had discovered that the Templar himself had sent to the Sultan of his own accord and had summoned the satrap. From this, he argued, the barbarians* <note>original: "barbaros"; a common contemporary term for the Saracen or Muslim forces</note> *had been made more courageous, because the Crusaders* <note>original: "cruce signati"; literally "those signed with the cross"</note> *seemed to be longing for peace and already thinking of their return home. Therefore, Saint Louis forbade the Barbarians to be received by the Templars, or to be heard, or for any exchange of conversation to be mixed between them.* Baronius <note>Cesare Baronio, an Italian cardinal and ecclesiastical historian.</note>, for the year 1248, number 9.

<margin>§. 121. Those not keeping treaties are pursued in war.</margin>

In the year 1263. *The Sultan of Babylon* <note>original: "Sultanus Babyloniæ"; in medieval texts, "Babylon" frequently refers to Cairo or the Egyptian Sultanate.</note> *Baibars* <note>original: "Bendocdar"; the Mamluk Sultan Baibars, known for his military campaigns against the Crusader states.</note> *came before Acre* <note>original: "Ptolomaydam"; the ancient name Ptolemais was commonly used for the city of Acre.</note> *with 30,000 cavalry on the 14th day of April: on the following day, he charged forward with great impetus even to the very gates of the city,*

<margin>§. 122. Almost all perish in the siege of Acre.</margin>

In the destruction of Acre <note>original: "Acconis"; the city of Acre was the last major Crusader stronghold in the Levant.</note>, or Ptolemais, in the year 1291, almost the entire order was extinguished by the Saracens. That city, then crowded with such countless people as no city in Italy could easily support today, was being oppressed and torn apart rather than governed by seven different and mutually antagonistic types of magistrates and nations. For the Patriarch, holding the title of Jerusalem <note>The Patriarch of Jerusalem, though the city was lost, maintained his seat in Acre as the leading ecclesiastical authority.</note>, sought to claim for himself temporal as well as spiritual governance; on one side the Templars, on the other the Hospitallers, and likewise the Teutonic Knights <note>original: "Teutonici"; the Order of Brothers of the German House of Saint Mary in Jerusalem.</note>, demanded the same authority based on their handling of arms and the shedding of blood, as they phrased it. ...regarding the dominion <note>This completes the word from the previous page's catchword "de-", likely referring to "dominium" or "regium"</note> looking solely to himself, the King of Cyprus desired it for the reason that three years earlier he had assumed the titles of the Kingdom of Jerusalem in opposition to the Count of Artois <note>Robert II, Count of Artois (1250–1302), who claimed the throne through his mother</note>, both of his own accord and with a few others supporting his rashness. These men were to preserve the rights of his father, grandfather, and great-grandfather in that city, even by force of arms. In this matter he became so inflamed with anger that he plundered and ravaged the estates of the Templars and Hospitallers within his Kingdom of Naples, having heard that they favored the Cypriot King.

the Sultan <note>referring to Al-Ashraf Khalil, the Mamluk Sultan of Egypt</note> threatened destruction. After repeatedly demanding—three and four times through messengers, but all in vain—the return of plundered cattle, captives, and other property, he finally set a deadline. He declared that unless the stolen goods were returned by that date, he would avenge the injury he had received through force of arms. Therefore, because the soldiers <note>the Crusaders and Templars</note>, who were mired in every kind of vice, held his threats in contempt, the Sultan waited until the year he had appointed for the restitution of property had passed. He then sent 150,000 armed men into Ptolemais <note>Acre</note> under the leadership of his son. This force overwhelmed and put to flight the garrison and the inhabitants who remained, and in the third month of the siege, they captured and destroyed the city by fire. <margin>§ 125.
The End of the Templars.</margin>
And so, we have brought this review of the expeditions which the Templars undertook—especially in Asia, but also in other regions of the world—down to those times nearest to when their order was destroyed and abolished. Because this was a most severe and highly memorable fate, we shall narrate it more distinctly at the end of this little book. We will speak first of the plan to extinguish the Templars, then of the execution of that plan, and finally, we will briefly add our own observations, which the shocking outcome of such a great matter naturally provides.

<margin>§ 126.

<meta>catchword: of all</meta>

<margin>§ 132. The significance of the Templars' case.</margin>
Whether the crimes of which the Templars were accused were true or fabricated is a question whose correct resolution contributes much to understanding the logic behind the judgments of God. <note>original: "judiciorum Dei rationes"</note> For if they were entirely innocent, it cannot be denied that the Roman Pontiff <note term="Roman Pontiff">The Pope; in this context, Clement V.</note> or the other leaders of the Church gathered in a Synod <note term="Synod">A council or assembly of high-ranking church officials convened to decide on matters of doctrine or administration.</note> sometimes weigh the cases of the accused with an unfair scale. It would mean they judge while being carried away by anger, hatred, envy, greed, or other passions, or are otherwise seized by ignorance, credulity, and suspicion. 

But if, in truth, they committed either all or some of the crimes of which they—whether as an entire group, as individuals, or as a majority—were accused, then those who complain have a powerful argument. Their argument would be that the Roman Catholic Church—including both its head <note>The Pope</note> and its members, both the clergy and the laity, and religious orders both monastic and military—sometimes lived a most shameful life, one incompatible with holy truth. This would serve as an unassailable argument <note>original: "invictum argumentum"</note> to confirm that part of the accusation. Finally, if this ill-fated order, without the [consent] of all... <note>The sentence ends abruptly due to the page break; the catchword "omnium" (of all) indicates the thought continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page explores the high stakes of the Templars' guilt or innocence, noting that their innocence would imply corruption or fallibility in the Papacy, while their guilt would prove deep moral decay within the medieval Church's institutions.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, Roman Pontiff, Synod, Church corruption, religious orders, divine judgment, heresy trials</keywords>

[whether it] was abolished because of the guilt of each and all, or was crushed by envy, power, and slander: he who re-examines the matter more deeply will easily gather from this that the Order's institution did not please God, and therefore neither the religious pilgrimages nor the insane desire for possessing Palestine <note>original: "Palæstinæ"</note> were pleasing or acceptable to Him.

<margin>§ 133. They are blamed by these authors:</margin>
It is difficult to offer anything certain from the testimonies of historians to define a question of such great weight; for they are praised by some, and blamed by others. *Flavius Blondus*, <note term="Flavius Blondus">Flavio Biondo (1392–1463), a Renaissance humanist historian famous for his work *Decades of History*, which was a primary source for later historians of the Middle Ages.</note> a writer contemporary with Pope Pius II, condemns them. *The source of a greater evil* [he writes in Decade II, book 9, under the year 1307], *the Templars were proven to be the most destructive of all many years earlier in the Asian wars waged against the infidels <note>The term "infidels" was used by medieval Christian writers to refer to Muslims.</note>; for it was established that they, despising and blaspheming Christ, and utterly mocking Him for whom they were accustomed to fight, had reached an agreement with the Turks and Saracens <note term="Saracens">A common medieval term for Muslims, particularly those in the Holy Land and surrounding regions.</note>, and had opposed the expeditions of our own people. Whence, many of them having been subjected to various punishments,*

# HISTORY

<margin>The state of the case.</margin>
...or to pile up testimonies regarding the unjust case of the Templars, which can be read in **Camerarius** <note term="Camerarius">Joachim Camerarius the Younger (1534–1598), a German scholar. But what seems to us in this matter to be true, what false, what certain, what uncertain, what likely, and likewise what unlikely: we shall now set forth briefly and with as much diligence as possible, for no other purpose than that we might rightly understand the past judgments of God, and learn to form more prudent opinions concerning the future.

<margin>§. 136. Luxury and lusts of the Templars.</margin>
First, therefore, I believe it is not only probable but also certain that the entire Order of the Templars, after it had achieved such immense growth, immersed itself in luxury, intemperance, drunkenness, immodesty, and lust—vices which wealth is usually prone to produce. For such at that time was the life of the Clergy, Monks, Canons, Bishops, Cardinals, and even the Roman Pontiffs themselves; regarding this, most well-attested complaints exist among many writers. Thus, if our [knights] <note>The text here cuts off at "mi-", likely beginning the word *milites*, meaning "soldiers" or "knights."</note> had lived more temperately, more chastely...

<margin>§. 137.
Male Venus.</margin>
Furthermore, one will easily believe that "male Venus" <note>A classical euphemism for homosexual acts, referencing the Roman goddess of love.</note> was not unknown to the Order of the Templars—who were not permitted to take a wife due to their sworn vow of continence. One may recall that the sin of Sodom, which was once punished by fire sent from heaven, was for a very long time regarded in France, Spain, and Italy as a mere "peccadillo" <note>original: "peccatillo"; a minor or trifling sin.</note>. I am ashamed to recount examples of this nefarious matter from the Annals, especially since it is an admitted fact among all people.

<margin>§. 138.
The Betrayal of Frederick II.</margin>
Thirdly, we do not deny that the Templars betrayed the Christian cause to the Saracens <note term="Saracens">A medieval term for Muslims, particularly those fighting against the Crusaders in the Holy Land.</note> on more than one occasion, whenever they feared for the preservation of their own power or the maintenance and increase of their wealth gathered from religious pilgrims. <margin>§. 139. Defection from Christ.</margin>
Fourthly, Puteanus <note term="Puteanus">Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar who published trial records to justify the suppression of the Templars.</note> proves from the Acts <note term="Acts">The official records of the trials of the Knights Templar (1307–1312).</note> that several persons constantly affirmed that they had denied Christ. <margin>§. 140. Not of all, but of some.</margin>
Fifthly, when we hold the Society of the Templars <term>Societas Templariorum</term> guilty of the crimes noted above, we by no means mean that these acts were committed by every single member, such that not even one innocent man existed among so many who were guilty. For even in the most corrupt assemblies, Orders, and Colleges, a merciful God is accustomed to preserve some from the widely spreading impiety, so that they might rebuke, exhort, and correct a world immersed in crimes, and by their own example invite others to repentance and the amendment of life. In this same way, we gladly acknowledge and give thanks to God that before the time of Luther <note term="Luther">Martin Luther (1483–1546), the leader of the Protestant Reformation; the author suggests that even before the Reformation, there were internal calls for reform.</note>, in the midst of a deluge <note>original: "colluvie"; literally a collection of filth or a cesspool, describing the moral state of the era.</note> of all crimes and corruptions, there lived pious Popes, Cardinals, Bishops, Priests, and Monks who deplored the most depraved state of the Church, and [their] brothers...

<summary>The author argues that while the Templar Order may have been guilty of crimes, it is unjust to assume every individual member was corrupt. He draws a parallel to the Catholic Church before the Reformation, noting that even in corrupt times, God preserves a pious few to act as a moral conscience.</summary>

# HISTORIA
190

...not being helped, and they saw that the Barbarians <note>In the context of the Crusades, the author uses "Barbarians" to refer to the non-Christian forces, such as the Mamluks or Turks, who were gaining military superiority over the Latin settlements.</note> were far superior in strength.

<margin>§. 139. Defection from Christ.</margin>
Fourthly, *Puteanus* <note term="Puteanus">Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar and King's librarian who published the records of the trial of the Templars.</note> proves from the *Acts* <note>The official trial records or judicial proceedings of the case against the Templars (1307–1312).</note> that some members consistently affirmed they had denied Christ. However, anyone who accuses the entire Order of a defection from the Christian religion on that basis does not reason any more correctly than one who concludes that the entire Venetian army <note term="Veneti">The people of Venice, a major maritime and military power often engaged in conflict with the Ottoman Empire.</note> had adopted Islamic <note>original: "Muhamedana"</note> rites because one or two soldiers from it deserted to the Turks and had themselves circumcised. Concerning the formula by which a person was to be received into the College <note term="College">Here referring to the organized body or Society of the Templars.</note>, besides the other articles, he was supposedly required to swear: *that he believed in God the Creator who was neither dead nor about to die* <note>original: "ſe credere in Deum Creatorem neque mortuum neque moriturum"</note>. I have nothing to say about this; nor is anything read in *Torsellus* <note term="Torsellus">Marino Sanudo Torsello (c. 1270–1343), a Venetian historian who documented the Crusades and the state of the Holy Land.</note> regarding the worship of a gilded head, the trampling of the cross, or the drinking of a potion made from the ashes of a dead body—even though he diligently recorded the pride, greed, robberies, and other vices of the Templars. Certainly to the first Christians, and later to those who from Rome—

<margin>§ 140. Not of all, but of some.</margin>

Fifthly, when we hold the Society of the Templars guilty of the crimes noted above, we by no means intend to suggest that these were committed by every single member, such that not even one innocent man existed among so many guilty ones. For in the most corrupt gatherings, Orders, and Colleges, a merciful God is accustomed to preserve some from the widely spreading impiety, so that they might rebuke, exhort, and correct a world immersed in crimes, and by their own example invite others to repentance and the amendment of life. In this same way, we gladly admit and give thanks to God that before the time of Luther <note term="Luther">Martin Luther (1483–1546), the German monk and theologian whose challenges to the Catholic Church initiated the Protestant Reformation.</note>, in the midst of a cesspool <note>original: "colluvie"</note> of all crimes and corruptions, there lived pious Popes, Cardinals, Bishops, Priests, and Monks, who deplored the most depraved state of the Church, and [their] brothers...

<margin>§ 141. The accusation, defense, and condemnation are considered.</margin>

Sixthly, if we look with more attentive eyes at the accusation, defense, and condemnation of the unfortunate defendants, we find arguments everywhere that tend to diminish their guilt. ->193<-

...I would say <note>original: "dicerem"; this completes the thought from the previous page regarding the accusers who were offered immunity.</note> that those thrown into prison, having been promised safety and immunity, denounced the Templars to Philip <note term="Philip">Philip IV of France (1268–1314), known as "the Fair," who was the primary driver behind the destruction of the Templar Order.</note>. Now, as far as the defense is concerned, some never acknowledged the crimes charged against the Order; others confessed, having been overcome by the force of torture and the fear of death, or were won over by money and promises—confessions they later revoked. Others, even when the King promised them safety if they would admit to the Order's crimes, steadfastly maintained its innocence, disregarding his promises. Finally, some even protested their innocence while in the midst of the flames, as can be read at length in *Puteanus* <note term="Puteanus">Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a scholar whose historical works provided detailed accounts of the Templar trials.</note>. The wording of the Bull <note term="Bulla">A Papal Bull, a formal decree issued by the Pope.</note> condemning the Order, which was promulgated in the second session of the Synod of Vienne <note term="Synod of Vienne">A church council (1311–1312) called by Pope Clement V, which ultimately suppressed the Order of the Templars.</note>, clearly proves that the strict requirements of the law were not followed in that case. It states that the Order was abolished by the Fathers gathered in the Council, *not by the form of a definitive sentence—since we could not legally issue one based on the inquiries and processes conducted regarding these matters—but by way of provision or ordinance* <note>original: "non per formam sententiæ definitivæ, cùm eam super hoc secundum inquisitiones & processus super his habitos non possemus ferre de jure, sed per viam provisionis seu ordinis"; This highlights a crucial legal distinction: the Pope admitted there was not enough evidence for a formal guilty verdict, so he used his administrative power to dissolve the group anyway.</note>

Philip—remembering that he had recently seen the man [Clement] prostrate at his feet and promising everything <note>A reference to the King's role in securing Clement's election and the Pope's perceived subservience to the French crown.</note>, and refusing to be looked down upon by Popes—replied to Clement with great vehemence. He argued that by taking away from the Bishops the ministry entrusted to them by God, the Pope was scorning God’s own representatives with a most grave sin and was acting to protect the wicked. Nevertheless, Philip sends one or two of the leading Templars to Poitiers <note>original: "Pictavium"</note>, where the Pontiff was then residing, so that they might be heard by Clement. Pacified by this gesture, the Pope either restores or confirms the power of the French theologians to examine the case of the accused, with the exception of the Grand Master of the Order and the Preceptors, who were reserved to be judged by the Apostolic See <note term="Apostolic See">The central authority of the Catholic Church in Rome (or at this time, Avignon), consisting of the Pope and his court.</note>.

Their end in France.
</margin>

But whatever the guilt of the Templars may have been, whether committed against God or men, the day was at hand, the hour of destruction had arrived. The year 1307 of the Common Era <note>original: "Æ. V. supra millesimum ter centesimus & septimus"</note> was unfolding—a fatal year for the order. The King, prudent and focused on the task, had sent letters throughout all of France with a mandate: they were all to be opened at the same hour everywhere, and the orders contained within them were to be carried out without delay. For he feared that the knights, who were flourishing in both authority and power, might prepare a defense if they were alerted to the danger. In Paris <note>Original: *Lutetiæ*.</note>, the Grand Master of the Temple, Jacques de Molay <note>Original: *Jacobus Mola*.</note> of Burgundy, was captured along with fifty-nine of his fellow knights. They were accused of heresy, unnatural lust <note>A frequent medieval accusation used to delegitimize groups, typically referring to sodomy.</note>, contempt for the cross, and treason <note>Original: *læfæ Majestatis*, literally "injured majesty," a crime against the sovereign.</note>. Wasted by long imprisonment, fifty-six of them were bound to as many stakes set at moderate distances from one another and were roasted over a slow fire. They declared that they died innocent, while their friends and relatives warned them in vain to accept the hope of a pardon offered by the King if they would only confess to the crime. (See Bzovius <note>Abraham Bzovius (1567–1637), a Polish Dominican historian known for continuing the *Annales Ecclesiastici*.</note> for the year 1307, number 4).

<margin>§ 149. Provincial Councils regarding the case of the Templars.</margin>
In the year 1309, at the command of Pope Clement [V], Provincial Councils <note>Regional meetings of bishops from a specific ecclesiastical province.</note> and Diocesan Synods were convened by holy men in almost every region. In these gatherings, investigations were launched against the Templars regarding the crimes with which they had been charged. Their property was seized by Papal Commissioners, and the funds generated from leasing their estates were placed into the treasury of the Roman Church for public needs (Bzovius <note>Abraham Bzovius, a historian who continued the *Annales Ecclesiastici*.</note>, no. 12). At Salamanca, in the territory of the Vettones <note>Latin: *Vectonibus*. A classical reference to the ancient people of the region around Salamanca, Spain.</note>, a council of the Church Fathers was held. Present were Rodrigo of Compostela <note>Rodrigo del Padrón, Archbishop of Santiago de Compostela.</note>, John of Lisbon, Vasco of Idanha <note>Bishop of Idanha-a-Velha, Portugal.</note>, Gonzalo of Zamora, Peter of Ávila, Alfonso of Ciudad Rodrigo, Dominic of Plasencia, Rodrigo of Mondoñedo, Alfonso of Astorga, John of Tui, and John of Lugo. Having conducted an inquiry concerning the prisoners and those seeking mercy, and having examined the case, they pronounced in favor of their innocence by common

<header>OF THE TEMPLARS.</header>

...who they were, several were gravely advising that those proven innocent should be acquitted by right, that the order should be preserved, and that so noble a member of the Church should not be severed. Others, on the contrary, judged that all should be destroyed and the memory of this most shameful Society abolished, claiming that nothing healthy, nothing whole or uncorrupted could be found within it. Pope Clement <note>Pope Clement V (reigned 1305–1314). He was the first pope to reside in Avignon and was under significant pressure from the French crown.</note> was among the first to urge this opinion vehemently, along with King Philip <note>Philip IV of France, also known as Philip the Fair. He initiated the trials against the Templars in 1307.</note>, who was present at the Council with his brother and his three sons. 

Therefore, in the second session, held on the 11th of the Kalends of April <note>March 22nd</note> in the year 1312, the Fathers decreed that the order of the Templars must be extinguished. The Bishop of Rome promulgated this sentence on the 6th of the Nones of May <note>May 2nd</note>, expressed in part with these words: 

*Indeed, for a long time, the Order of the house of the knighthood of the temple of Jerusalem <note>original: "Ordinem domus militiæ templi Hierofolymitani"</note>—on account of the Master and the Brothers, and other persons of the said order residing in various parts of the world—[has been involved in] various and diverse, not so much nefarious as unspeakable (alas!) obscenities of errors and crimes—* <note>This quote is the beginning of the papal bull *Vox in excelso*, which suppressed the Templar Order. The text ends mid-word on "obfcæ-", likely for "obscenities."</note>

*...bespattered with obscenities, depravities, stains, and blemishes, which we pass over in silence in the present document because of their sad and filthy memory; and the state, habit, and name of that Order, not without bitterness of heart and grief, with the approval of the holy Council, not by way of a definitive sentence <note>A "definitive sentence" is a formal legal judgment of guilt. Pope Clement V famously suppressed the Templars through administrative "provision" rather than a judicial verdict because the evidence of heresy was legally insufficient for a formal conviction of the entire Order.</note>, since we could not legally pass such a sentence based on the Inquisitions and processes held regarding these matters, but by way of provision or Apostolic ordinance, we have abolished by an irrefragable and eternally valid sanction, subjecting it to a perpetual prohibition, strictly forbidding anyone from entering the said Order in the future, or receiving or wearing its habit, or presuming to act as a Templar: should anyone act to the contrary, they would incur the sentence of excommunication <note>Excommunication: the formal exclusion of a person from the sacramental life of the Church.</note> by that very fact.*

<term>Templars</term> — Members of the Poor Fellow-Soldiers of Christ and of the Temple of Solomon, a Catholic military order.
<term>Order</term> — A lineage of communities and institutions established in the Catholic Church.
<term>Council</term> — Refers here to the Council of Vienne (1311–1312), which was called to deal with the Templars.
<term>Inquisition</term> — The formal ecclesiastical inquiry or legal process into the charges of heresy.
<term>Excommunication</term> — A censure by which a member of the church is deprived of spiritual privileges.
<term>Property of the Templars</term> — The vast network of lands, castles, and wealth owned by the order, which became a primary focus of European monarchs following the suppression.

With the consent of the bishops of his province, the Archbishop acquitted the accused of the charges, according to *Nauclerus, Generation 44.* <note>Johannes Nauclerus (1425–1510) was a historian and author of a universal chronicle.</note> Only the name and the rules of the Order were changed; the members and their revenues were distributed among other holy religious orders. 

As for why Clement was more merciful toward the Templars of the Mainz Province than toward others, I believe it happened because he held Archbishop Peter in singular affection for restoring his health, and also because a sense of weariness or perhaps horror had finally overcome his mind regarding such a great slaughter—a destruction carried out more by the King's <note>King Philip IV "the Fair" of France, who orchestrated the downfall of the Templars.</note> will and judgment than by his own.

<margin>Section 153. History of the last Master of the Templars.</margin>

The last Master of the Templars <note>Jacques de Molay (c. 1240–1314), the 23rd and final Grand Master of the Knights Templar.</note> was not so fortunate as to escape the punishment of death along with the others; rather, the common disaster consumed him. It is fitting to subjoin his history at the end. He was born into a most illustrious family in Burgundy within the diocese of Besançon <note>original: "Bisuntinensi dioecesi." The Archdiocese of Besançon is located in eastern France.</note>, but he was poor because, by French law, all the paternal wealth had passed to his elder brother. Every author I have consulted calls him *Jacques de Molay*, or Mola; however, Charles du Fresne <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange (1610–1688), a famous French scholar and lexicographer.</note> notes in his *Glossary* that he descended from the lineage of the Lords *of Nolay*. 

Seeking to throw off the yoke of his first-born brother and to prepare a path to wealth and honors for himself through his own courage, he joined the knighthood of the Temple. In this Order, he was first appointed *Prior*, and then, after the death of *Monachus Gaudin* <note>Thibaud Gaudin, the Grand Master who oversaw the retreat from the Holy Land in 1291.</note>, he was elected *Master* of the Order upon the recommendation of Princes. He conquered Tortosa <note>A strategic coastal fortress in modern-day Syria, one of the last Crusader outposts.</note> together with Almeric, the Lord of Tyre <note>Amalric, Prince of Tyre and brother to the King of Cyprus.</note>, and from that time he bravely waged war against the Saracens <note>A term used by medieval Europeans to refer to Muslim forces.</note> for several years, until he was driven out by the Babylonian Sultan <note>original: "Babylonico Sultano." In this period, "Babylon" was frequently used to refer to Cairo, the seat of the Mamluk Sultanate.</note> in...

<margin>Section 154. The death of the authors of the Templars' destruction.</margin>
The authors of the Templars’ destruction—the Pope at Avignon and the King of France—did not survive its downfall for long. Indeed, Clement V <note>Pope Clement V (reigned 1305–1314), the first Pope of the "Avignon Captivity" who moved the papal court to France.</note> ended his final day on April 20th <note>original: "ad XII. <margin>§. 155. Where an Order has its beginning and growth, it often also has its end.</margin>
The consideration of the destruction of the Templars just recounted spontaneously provides various observations that should not be overlooked, whether we look at the authors of such a great disaster, its causes, or the disaster itself. For the primary author was the King of France, whose example other Kings and Princes followed. Noble Frenchmen founded the Order; a Synod assembled in France confirmed it; and the Kings of France increased it with benefits and dignities. Yet, it was Philip the Fair, <note>Philip IV of France (reigned 1285–1314), whose conflict with the Papacy and need for funds led to the Order's suppression.</note> one of the most glorious Kings of France, who destroyed and overturned it. Indeed, societies and the leaders of societies often meet their end where they took their beginning and growth, once their fated time has elapsed. Just as Saint John saw ten Kings [give] their strength and power to the Beast... <note>A reference to the Book of Revelation 17:12-13, suggesting a biblical parallel to the shifting alliances of earthly rulers.</note>

<margin>Section 158. Wealth is harmful to Monks and Clerics.</margin>
Wealth, luxury, and the pride born from them hurried the Templars toward their destruction. These things quite often incited the anger of Kings, especially the English, against Monks and Clerics. ...stantinople, he also contemplated invading the **Byzantine Empire** <note>original: "Constantinopolitano... imperio"; referring to the Eastern Roman Empire.</note> and expelling **Michael Palaeologus** <note>Michael VIII, the Byzantine Emperor who had recently recaptured Constantinople from the Latins.</note>, and he coveted **Italy** in his capacity as a **Roman Senator** and **Imperial Vicar**. But domestic uprisings stood in the way of these great and ambitious efforts. For since the governors and those **Frenchmen** who administered other offices throughout **Sicily** in his name were treating the citizens unjustly, and were venting their fury both upon the virtue of the women and upon the fortunes and lives of the men, a plan was undertaken to reclaim the island's liberty from French servitude. 

**Pandulfo Collenuccio** <note>A 15th-century Italian historian.</note> praises *John of Procula from Salerno*, the physician of **Manfred**, as the architect of such a great deed; **Flavio Biondo** calls him *John of Procida*, and **Platina** calls him *Prochyta*. **Mézeray** <note>A French historian.</note> writes that he was the Lord of **Procida**, an island in the **Tyrrhenian Sea** located not far from **Pozzuoli**, a town in **Campania**, but had been driven out by **Charles** on charges of treason. Whoever he may have been, once the matter was deliberated with the nobles of **Sicily**, he first set out for **Constantinople**

<header>OF THE TEMPLARS.</header>

He revealed Charles’s plans to the Emperor <note>Michael VIII Palaeologus, the Byzantine Emperor in Constantinople.</note>; then, hurrying to **Catalonia**, he offered the kingdom of the island <note>Sicily</note> to **Peter of Aragon**, the husband of **Constance**, who was the daughter of **Manfred** <note>Manfred was the previous King of Sicily, defeated by Charles of Anjou; Peter of Aragon claimed the throne through his wife, Manfred's daughter.</note>. Having also undertaken two journeys to **Rome**, he brought over to his side **Pope Nicholas III**, a member of the **Orsini** family. This Pope not only envied Charles's power—which was formidable to his neighbors—but was also angry because, when he had requested Charles's daughter in marriage for his own nephew, he had been dismissed by Charles with mockery <note>original: "risu"; literally "laughter." Charles considered a marriage alliance with the Pope's family to be beneath his royal status.</note>. **Procida** <note>referred to in the text as "Procula."</note> made these journeys dressed in a monk's habit, and he managed the business so shrewdly and wisely for eighteen full months that the **French** <note>original: "Galli."</note> did not have the slightest suspicion of the danger before it broke upon them. The **Sicilians** had agreed that on **Easter Monday**, which at that time fell on the 30th of March in the year 1281 <note>Most historical records date the Sicilian Vespers to 1282; the author here uses 1281.</note>, towards evening, at the first sound of the bell <note>The bell for Vespers, or evening prayers.</note>, they would each slaughter the **French** they found among them or encountered anywhere, making no distinction of sex. Nor did they fail their pledge; indeed, when the appointed day and hour arrived, they unexpectedly [attacked] the **French**...

They fall upon them, and throughout the entire island, within the space of two hours, they slaughter everyone, with not even a single person escaping death. They were burning with such great anger or cruelty that if they knew of any pregnant **French** women, they pierced the mothers along with their unborn children with the sword, so that nothing of **French** blood might remain in **Sicily**, while **King Charles** himself was staying at **Naples**.

<margin>§. 161.
*The Parisian*
*Slaughter.*</margin>
To the **Sicilian Vespers** it is pleasing to subjoin the **French Matins** <note>original: "Matutinas Gallicas." This is a play on words: "Vespers" are evening prayers, while "Matins" are early morning prayers, here used to describe the St. Bartholomew's Day Massacre.</note>, as some have called the **Parisian Slaughter** <note>original: "Lanienam Pariſienſem"; referring to the mass killing of Huguenots in 1572.</note>, which was all the more horrific because those butchered were more innocent. Since the **Evangelicals** <note>The Huguenots, or French Protestants.</note> in **France** could neither be extinguished by war nor deterred from their devotion to the "new religion" (as it was called) by punishments, **King Charles** <note>Charles IX of France.</note> decided to destroy all the **Protestants** of his kingdom in a common slaughter. This was after he had first entered into a treaty with **Philip II**, King of the **Spains**, through the mediation of his wife **Elizabeth** and **Charles’s** sister <note>Elizabeth of Valois, who was Charles IX's sister and Philip II's wife.</note>, in which they swore to the confirmation of the **Council of Trent** <note>The Catholic Church's formal response to the Protestant Reformation, which established strict doctrines.</note>. Curious observers have noted that the first deliberation concerning that massacre, with **Guise** <note>Henry I, Duke of Guise, a leader of the Catholic faction in France.</note> especially...

<meta>catchword: especially</meta>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the St. Bartholomew’s Day Massacre (1572), specifically describing the secret councils held to plan the slaughter and the king’s initial hesitation.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>
<header>OF THE TEMPLARS.</header> <note>Though the header mentions the Templars, the text continues the history of the French Wars of Religion; this likely refers to the broader section of the book or is a printer's error.</note>
<sig>P 3</sig>

...instigated by the [Queen Mother] herself, was held at **Blois**, in the very same chamber in which **Henry** <note>King Henry III of France.</note> later ordered the **Duke** <note>Henry I, Duke of Guise, assassinated in 1588.</note> to be slaughtered. The other council, however, over which the **Duke of Anjou** <note>The future Henry III.</note> (brother of **Charles** and later his successor) presided, was established in the **Gondi** house at **Saint-Cloud**—a place later defiled by the murder of **King Henry** perpetrated by the monk **Jacques Clément**.

In order to draw the **Protestants** <note>original: "Evangelicos"; referring to the Huguenots.</note> more surely into his trap, **Charles** treated them with the greatest kindness. He revered the venerable old man **Coligny**, the head of their faction, as if he were a father; he betrothed his sister **Margaret** to **Henry of Navarre**, who was then still devoted to the **Reformed religion**; and he invited the leading men from across the entire kingdom to the wedding. He acted so convincingly that the **Pope** almost believed the King had become a **Huguenot** himself. 

However, as the time destined for completing this detestable plan approached, he was troubled in his mind not a little, and might perhaps have rejected the plans he had undertaken, had his mother **Catherine** <note>Catherine de' Medici.</note> not repeatedly warned him that it is far better for the "rotten members" to be cut off than for the "breast of the Church" to be torn any longer: 

<margin>retur:</margin> <note>Catchword from the original Latin "laceretur" (be torn).</note>

<summary>This page details the deceptive measures taken by King Charles IX and Catherine de' Medici to lure Huguenot leaders to Paris, including the marriage of Henry of Navarre, and describes the King's moral hesitation before the massacre.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the St. Bartholomew’s Day Massacre in 1572. <page-num>230</page-num>
<header>HISTORY</header>

...torn; although both she herself and the **Duke of Anjou** <note>The future King Henry III of France.</note> obtained with difficulty his permission for the signal to start the slaughter to be given at the impending hour. Indeed, when the signal had already been given—and sooner than had been agreed upon, thanks to the arrangements of the **Queen Mother**, **Henry**, and **Guise** <note>Henry I, Duke of Guise, a staunch Catholic leader.</note>—the King sought to have the planned expedition delayed a little, but his prayers were in vain because the rest of the faction fought against his hesitation. The massacre began in **Paris** <note>original: "Lutetia".</note> with the killing of **Coligny** <note>Gaspard de Coligny, the Admiral of France and leader of the Huguenots.</note>, whose severed head was sent to the **Pope** in Rome. His bride **Margaret** <note>Margaret of Valois, the King's sister.</note> saved **Henry of Navarre**, and his kinsman **Nevers** <note>Louis de Gonzague, Duke of Nevers.</note> rescued the Prince of **Condé** <note>Henri I de Bourbon, a major Protestant leader.</note> from danger, on the condition that they would attend Mass; but all the others, upon whom the assassins and executioners fell, were slaughtered. Other cities of France followed the example of Paris and raged with the sword against the **Protestants** <note>original: "Evangelicos"; the term for the Huguenots who sought to return to the "Evangelical" or Gospel teachings.</note>, so that within a very few hours on a single day—the day sacred to **St. Bartholomew** in the year 1572—over one hundred thousand people of both sexes perished, and the rivers were stained with the innocent blood of the wretched victims.

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page serves as the concluding prayer and exhortation of the work, following the harrowing account of the St. Bartholomew’s Day Massacre. While the previous page ended mid-sentence describing the staggering death toll of the Protestants, this page opens by calling upon a higher power—likely God—to redirect the "governance of this world" away from such bloodshed toward a state of justice.</meta>

<page-num>231</page-num>
<header>OF THE TEMPLARS. 231</header>

...the governance <term>moderamen</term> <note>original: "moderamen"; referring to the divine steering or management of worldly affairs.</note> of this world, may He turn it from cruelty and injustice toward equity. May He mend our spirits with the sword and fire of the Holy Word, lest we deserve to be punished more harshly or even cut off. Furthermore, may He spread the Holy Gospel far and wide throughout the entire world. In this way, by its light, this earth—now cursed by such horrors—will finally be illuminated, and the unbelieving people <note>A reference to the Jewish people, reflecting the common 16th and 17th-century theological hope for their conversion following the "slaying of the Son of God."</note>, who are exiled and banished, shall build and guard a temple to God and the Messiah: a heart that is pure, fertile in truth, and devoted to piety!

->THE END.<-

<vocab>governance, equity, Holy Gospel, Messiah, temple, piety</vocab>
